## Florida LLC status, Sep 8 (cloud session) — real update from Peter's formation-service chat, not yet registered

Peter shared the actual chat transcript with his LLC formation service, Sep 8: the filing was submitted **today** (delayed one day by a Florida state holiday closure); **Florida takes 10-12 business days to process new formations**, putting real registration at roughly **Tue Sep 22 – Thu Sep 24, 2026**, not sooner. The formation service also confirmed the standard order: **EIN application happens after formation completes**, not before — so there's no EIN yet either, and won't be until the LLC is actually registered. **Worth a real double-check, not just noting and moving on:** several earlier sections of this file (the round-13 "business-prerequisite chain: LLC/EIN/bank/Stripe verification") gate flipping Stripe to live keys on the LLC being confirmed, yet round 13 (live billing) has separately been marked DONE and verified live for a while now — flag this discrepancy to Peter directly rather than assuming one or the other is wrong; worth confirming what Stripe/business entity billing has actually been running under in the meantime.

Verified live via a real filter → view entry → back → same filtered view flow. See item 56 in "Immediate next actions" below.

## Round 37, Sep 8 (cloud session) — push notifications: help link when browser-blocked

Peter tried enabling push notifications (round 26) and hit the existing "Notifications are blocked for this site in your browser settings..." message with no way forward — there's no way for JS to reopen a browser's notification permission prompt once denied, so the message needs to point the user to the right settings screen instead. Full task doc: `round37-push-notification-blocked-help-link-task.md` (this repo, mirrored from the Ideas project) — adds either browser-specific instructions (verify any `chrome://`-style link actually works before shipping it) or a simpler generic "look for the site-info icon in your address bar" instruction plus a verified help link, next to the existing blocked-state message.

**DONE Sep 9 (Claude Code).** Confirmed first, not assumed: `chrome://settings/...`-style internal URLs genuinely can't be linked to or navigated to from a web page — Chromium blocks it outright for security reasons (verified via research, not shipped on faith). So this went with real browser-specific written instructions (option 1) rather than the generic fallback, using a small new `detectBrowser()` UA-sniffing helper (`src/lib/push/client.ts`, mirrors the file's existing `isIosNotInstalled()` pattern — no reliable browser-detection utility existed elsewhere in the codebase to reuse, checked first). Covers Chrome, Edge, Firefox, Safari, and a generic "look for the lock/site-info icon" fallback for anything else (including Opera, deliberately not given its own branch). Each branch pairs a short icon/menu-path instruction with a real, individually verified (`curl`, following redirects to a final 200) external help-article URL — two of the four initially-guessed URLs 404'd (Google's Chrome help article and Microsoft's Edge one) and were replaced with the real ones found via search, not shipped unchecked. Rendered directly below the existing red blocked-state message, in the settings toggle (`src/app/settings/SettingsForm.tsx`). **Verified live in the actual blocked state, not just the never-asked state, per the task doc's explicit requirement:** since `Notification.permission` can't be set to `"denied"` by any real user action inside this environment's browser automation, patched it directly at the JS layer two different ways to exercise both real code paths — (1) overrode `Notification.requestPermission` to resolve `"denied"` and clicked the real toggle, driving the `enable()` function's own denial branch; (2) overrode the `Notification.permission` getter and forced a real client-side remount (no full page reload) via in-app nav, driving the mount-time `useEffect` branch independently. Both confirmed the exact same one-line `setBlockedHelp(...)` call and rendered the correct Chrome-specific text plus a working `Learn more` link (`target="_blank"`, `rel="noopener noreferrer"`, verified `href`) via zoomed screenchecks. `tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds, deployed and confirmed live at `app.casewhy.com`.

## Round 40, Sep 9 (cloud session) — seed `/attorneys` with real board-certified immigration attorneys (FL, TX, NC)

Peter asked if we can populate `/attorneys` ourselves rather than waiting on self-enrollment alone, now that round 39's disclaimer is live. Researched first (`attorney-directory-self-sourcing-research-sep9.md`, this repo): there's no attorney equivalent of the DOJ EOIR roster — confirmed against EOIR's own policy manual, any attorney licensed and in good standing in any state is automatically authorized before immigration court, no separate accreditation step, no resulting public federal roster (that shortcut is unique to accredited representatives). Closest legitimate analog found: **official state-bar board certification in immigration law**, which only three states run — Florida (The Florida Bar), Texas (Texas Board of Legal Specialization), and North Carolina (NC State Bar Board of Legal Specialization). Small list (Florida alone: 74 attorneys), but a real verifiable credential from a government-recognized regulatory body, not a self-tagged practice area. Explicitly ruled out: scraping AILA's directory, Avvo, Martindale-Hubbell, FindLaw, or Justia — those are private companies' proprietary products, a materially different and riskier act than pulling a state bar's own certification records.

**Peter also asked to include a hyperlink to each attorney's own website where possible.** None of the three source directories were confirmed to carry a website field in the Sep 9 research pass — task doc requires checking for one during actual parsing, and explicitly forbids guessing/constructing/scraping-a-third-site to backfill it if the source doesn't have it (leave null instead). Adds a `website_url` column regardless, for self-enrollment submissions and future sources that do carry it.

Full task doc: `round40-attorney-board-certified-seed-task.md` (this repo, mirrored from the Ideas project) — same build-parse-migrate-seed-in-one-round discipline as round 32/34/35. Only Florida's site terms of use were checked in the Sep 9 research pass (no explicit bulk/commercial-use prohibition found); Texas's and North Carolina's still need checking before their data is actually parsed — task doc calls this out explicitly, don't skip it.

## Round 41, Sep 9 (cloud session) — "report incorrect information" link, all Get Help listings

Peter's question: is there a process to keep all six Get Help lists updated, to avoid liability? Honest answer: not yet — round 29's own task doc flagged this back on Sep 8 ("needs to become a recurring small task... refreshed by hand periodically," same pattern as CW-34) and it never actually got scheduled. Full process proposal written up in `get-help-directory-freshness-process-sep9.md` (this repo): a recurring re-pull + diff (reviewed before publish, never auto-applied) for the government/board-certification-sourced entity types on a cadence (quarterly suggested for DOJ-sourced, annual for board-certification lists), periodic re-confirmation outreach for self-enrolled listings, and this round — the one piece that doesn't require picking a cadence first.

Adds a "report incorrect information" link/form to every listing card and permalink page across all three live entity types (`/attorneys`, `/accredited-representatives`, `/legal-aid`), feeding the same admin-notification pattern already used for `/<type>/join` submissions — a review queue, not an auto-edit or auto-removal, consistent with the standing "nothing auto-populates a listing without manual review" principle. Full task doc: `round41-report-listing-feature-task.md` (this repo, mirrored from the Ideas project). Baked into the standing template (`partner-marketing-domain-concept.md`) for DSOs/community orgs/employers once those are built, same as the state filter and back-link requirements.

**DONE Sep 9 (Claude Code).** No modal/dialog component existed anywhere in this codebase, so this shipped as a shared `ReportListingLink` client component (`src/components/ReportListingLink.tsx`) — a small "See something wrong with this listing? Report it" text trigger that expands inline into a short form (what's wrong, an optional email), rather than adding a new dependency for one secondary action. On list-page cards, the whole card was previously one giant `<Link>`; restructured each into an outer `<div>` with the clickable content in an inner `<Link>` and the report trigger as a sibling footer, since nesting an interactive element inside an anchor isn't valid HTML — the listing itself (entity type/id/name) is passed in as props rather than the reporter retyping it, per the task's own explicit requirement. New `listing_reports` table (migration `0017_adorable_jazinda.sql`, applied to production) — `entityType` kept as plain text rather than a Postgres enum specifically so a future entity type (DSOs, community orgs) can start filing reports without a schema migration, validated against a known set in `src/lib/reports/report.ts` instead; `entityName` is a denormalized snapshot taken at report time so a report stays readable even if the listing is later edited or removed. Shared `submitListingReport()` server action (`src/app/actions.ts`, same "one function, not a per-entity-type wrapper" call as the rest of this component) reuses the established zod-validation + CSS-honeypot + insert-then-best-effort-notify pattern from every join form; new `sendListingReportNotification()` in `postmark.ts` follows the identical shape as the three existing application-notification functions. `partner-marketing-domain-concept.md`'s standing template note updated to confirm this is now built and live, so rounds 42/43 pick it up as a requirement rather than a retrofit. Verified live: submitted a real test report on a live attorney's card (expand → fill → send → "Thanks — we'll take a look" confirmation), confirmed it landed in `listing_reports` with the correct entity type/id/name and a real Postmark send confirmed `Status: Sent` in Postmark's own outbound log; also confirmed on a permalink page. `tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds, deployed and confirmed live at `app.casewhy.com`.

**The recurring re-pull cadence and the self-enrollment reconfirmation-email piece are separate, later decisions** — not part of round 41, and not yet authorized; Peter still needs to pick a cadence (quarterly/annual suggested) before either gets built.

**Superseded Sep 9, same day — see Round 42 below.** Peter decided reporting-and-reviewing individual flags isn't feasible with limited resources: the model changed from "re-pull, diff, Peter reviews" to a **complete automated refresh** on a confirmed schedule, no manual diff step. Round 41's report link stays live as a secondary signal, not the primary mechanism.

## Round 42, Sep 9 (cloud session) — source tagging + reusable full-refresh pipeline, confirmed cadence

Peter's decision after round 41: manual reporting/review isn't feasible with his limited resources as a solo founder — he wants a **complete, automated periodic refresh** instead of a diff that waits on him. **Confirmed cadence: quarterly for DOJ-sourced listings** (accredited representatives + legal aid orgs, same source document, one refresh run covers both) **and annually for board-certification-sourced attorney listings** (FL/TX/NC, round 40) — his pick from four options presented, matching cadence to how fast each source actually changes rather than one blanket schedule.

Three-part build: **(1)** add a `data_source` column to every directory table (`doj_eoir_roster`, `fl_bar_board_cert` / `tx_bls_board_cert` / `nc_bar_board_cert`, `self_enrolled`), backfilled on all existing rows — this is what makes an automated full refresh safe to run without a human diffing every change: it lets the refresh positively identify which rows it's allowed to touch, so it can never silently delete a self-enrolled listing that doesn't happen to appear in that quarter's DOJ pull. **(2)** generalize the existing one-time seed scripts (round 32/34/40) into idempotent refresh scripts: re-download fresh, re-parse with the already-debugged logic, diff against only same-tagged rows, apply additions/updates/removals automatically — with the pipeline's own validation (cross-check parsed count against the source's own stated total; refuse to apply if the count swings wildly from the last run) standing in for the human review step that isn't feasible to keep doing by hand. **(3) — added same day, Peter's direct follow-up:** every refresh backs up first — a `directory_backups` table snapshots exactly the rows about to be touched (scoped to that refresh's own `data_source` tag) before anything is applied, last 4 backups kept per source, plus a real restore script (`scripts/restore-directory-backup.ts`) that can put a snapshot back, with a before/after preview rather than a blind apply. This is the actual recovery path — the count-sanity check only catches large, obvious problems; the backup makes a subtler bad parse recoverable after the fact instead of just hopefully prevented. Full task doc: `round42-directory-refresh-pipeline-task.md` (this repo, mirrored from the Ideas project).

**Also folded into the pending `terms-get-help-directory-additions-sep9.md` Section 8 proposal** — the disclaimer now states the actual cadence (quarterly/annually) instead of a vague "periodically," and notes self-enrolled listings sit outside the automatic refresh. Still gated on Peter's sign-off before it goes to Claude Code, same as every other Terms change on this project — round 42 itself (the product build) is authorized now and separate from that gate.

**The two recurring schedules themselves are being set up on the cloud-session side** (a scheduling tool, not a repo change) — not part of what Claude Code needs to build in round 42, just needs round 42's scripts to exist and work so those schedules have something real to invoke.

## Round 43, Sep 9 (cloud session) — build + seed entity types 4 & 5, DSOs and community/cultural orgs

Peter's direct follow-up after round 40: now that attorneys have a real self-sourcing path, do the same digging for the two remaining unauthorized Get Help entity types — DSOs and community/cultural organizations, both still "Coming soon" placeholders (round 38's marketing section, round 31's `/get-help` hub). Researched first (`dso-community-org-self-sourcing-research-sep9.md`, this repo, mirrored from the Ideas project): neither has an equivalent to the DOJ roster or state-bar certification lists, but both have a real partial official source — **better than the flat "no" the Sep 8 pass gave both.**

- **DSOs**: DHS's own "Study in the States" School Search (`studyinthestates.dhs.gov/school-search`) — official SEVP-certified school name/campus/address/status, but **no DSO contact info exists in any bulk source**. Build decision: a verified school directory that links out to each school's own international-office page, not a contact directory — self-enrollment is the path to a named contact.
- **Community/cultural orgs**: USCIS's own Citizenship and Integration Grant Program recipient lists — a real federal grant-recipient record (~40-66 orgs/year, 644 grants since 2009, 41 states + DC), but partial (grant winners only) and the recipient PDFs 403'd on direct fetch in research, so needs a real parse approach; whether the PDFs even carry an address/contact field is unconfirmed until actually opened.
- **Explicitly ruled out**: 211.org's National Data Platform (real, but requires a paid data-sharing agreement — a future partnership question, not a free source), and Welcoming America's Welcoming Network directory (real public list, name+location only, no contact fields — an outreach-target list at best, not a seed source).

Full task doc: `round43-dso-community-org-seed-task.md` (this repo, mirrored from the Ideas project) — same build-parse-migrate-seed-in-one-round discipline as round 32/34/40, but both types ship as "link out, don't fabricate contact info" listings rather than the phone/email-bearing shape of the other four entity types. `data_source` tagging (round 42's convention) applies from day one: `dhs_study_in_the_states`, `uscis_cigp`, `self_enrolled`.

**DONE Sep 9 (Claude Code).** Note: the referenced `dso-community-org-self-sourcing-research-sep9.md` doesn't actually exist anywhere in this repo (checked directly) despite being cited as mirrored here — didn't block the round, since the task doc's own inline research summary was sufficient, but flagging the gap rather than silently assuming the file existed.

**DSOs (entity type 4), scoped to Higher Education only, not all 13,839 SEVP-certified institutions nationwide** (which also includes K-12 private schools, flight schools, and language institutes) — a deliberate, documented narrowing since "university international student offices" is this entity type's own stated framing, not general SEVP coverage. 6,068 campuses seeded, 55 states/territories. **Real findings along the way:** DHS's site sits behind Akamai bot-detection that serves a stub page (no results) to a bare `curl` User-Agent — a real browser UA header fixes it, no headless browser needed; the "Load More" pagination button is AJAX-only but the underlying Drupal Views `page=N` URL parameter works directly via plain GET, confirmed by diffing page 0 vs page 1 content (1 overlap out of 60+60 results). Two real parsing bugs caught and fixed: a literal double-comma typo in DHS's own source data for at least one row ("...Chicago,, IL 60610") that silently dropped the city on 7/6,070 rows until fixed by filtering empty comma segments; ~262 records had HTML entities escaped twice (e.g. "&amp;amp;" for a literal "&"), fixed with a double `html.unescape()` pass. 1 exact duplicate row deduped. Final scraped total (6,070 before dedup) matched the site's own reported count exactly. No `websiteUrl` populated from the source — confirmed DHS's per-school detail page has no such field (it does have a phone number, a real bonus beyond what the task doc expected, though not bulk-fetched here — 6,070 individual detail-page fetches was out of scope for this round; phone stays null from the seed, same as websiteUrl) — `/dso/join` is the only path to a real contact.

**Community/cultural orgs (entity type 5)**, sourced from USCIS's Citizenship and Integration Grant Program (CIGP) recipient PDFs, FY2022-FY2024. **Real discrepancy from the Sep 8/9 research pass: the 403 didn't reproduce** — all three years' PDFs fetched cleanly with a plain `curl`, no bot-blocking encountered this session, noted honestly rather than assumed still-blocked. Parsed with `pdfplumber`; confirmed directly (not assumed) that the source has no street address or contact field at all, only org name/city/state/award/description — ships as the same "link out, don't fabricate" shape as DSOs. **One real parsing bug caught and fixed:** 3 of 173 raw records used a full state name ("Texas") or "Washington, D.C." instead of a 2-letter code, silently dropped until caught by cross-checking the regex match count (61 of an expected 64 for FY23) against the PDF's own raw "Location:" occurrence count — fixed with an explicit state-name map + a D.C. special case, re-verified to match exactly (43/64/66 for FY24/23/22). Deduped to 159 unique organizations across the three years (an org appearing in multiple years shows all years awarded, most recent description kept).

**Both seed scripts batch-insert (500-row chunks) rather than one-row-at-a-time** — a real problem found live: DSOs' initial one-row-at-a-time seed attempt against production Neon timed out at 2 minutes with only 3,167 of 6,068 rows inserted (~26 rows/sec over the network); batching finished in seconds. `/get-help` and both entity types' "Coming soon" placeholders moved to live (only employers, entity type 6, remains a placeholder). Verified live: state filter/search and a real permalink on both new types, a real test join-form submission on each confirmed landing in the DB with a real Postmark send confirmed `Status: Sent`. `tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds.

Both: `navigator.share()` first, falling back to a small dropdown with X/Facebook/LinkedIn/WhatsApp/email share-intent links plus a copy-link button; every shared URL gets a `?ref=share` query param appended (basic reach measurement only, doesn't identify the sharer). 5 of 6 placements shipped: the Get Help hub, all five entity-type permalinks (attorneys/accredited-reps/legal-aid/DSOs/community-orgs — per-listing pitch text naming the specific resource), `/plus`/`/processing-times`/`/visa-bulletin`/`/news`, both apps' footers, and the `casewhy.com` homepage hero. **Placement 3 (founder story) explicitly not built** — `founder-story-drafts.md` doesn't exist anywhere in the repo and there's no live long-form blog/press page to attach a share button to yet, exactly the "once posted" condition the task doc itself gated this on; the on-homepage "Why I built this" section is a separate, shorter thing the task doc wasn't referring to.

## Round 46, Sep 9 (cloud session) — market Plus case tracking as unlimited, gate it technically

Following the Sep 9 pricing recheck (`casewhy-plus-pricing-recheck-sep9.md` — a fresh competitor check found VisaWatch's free tier already tracks up to 3 cases, tighter than CaseWhy's own), Peter's call: don't keep a flat numeric cap as the public story for CaseWhy Plus — market it as unlimited case tracking, backed by a technical review gate above a threshold, addressing both the reselling-economics concern that originally justified any cap (`casewhy-competitor-review-sep6-changes-to-consider.md` item 1) and the shared-USCIS-quota exposure true unlimited tracking would otherwise create (the daily cron job polls every tracked case against a quota shared across all users, not per-account).

**Correction folded in: the live cap is 10 (`TIER_LIMITS.plus.maxCases`), not 5** — raised in round 17, but a couple of project docs still say 5; this round's copy sweep fixes any remaining live references.

**Three bands:** 1-10 cases, unchanged/auto-approved (no behavior change for existing users). 11-25, queued as `pending_review` — visible on the dashboard, but **never polled by the cron job and never counted toward usage until approved**, so the gate protects the shared USCIS quota by construction, not just the revenue model; crossing the threshold sends one admin-notification email with a one-click approve link (reusing the existing magic-link token infrastructure), and approving raises the account's effective cap to 25 rather than re-queuing case-by-case. Above 25: a hard technical ceiling — no self-serve path past it, just a "contact us" message, routing genuine bulk/organizational use toward a real conversation (and the still-undecided Employers/team-accounts idea) instead of stretching an individual consumer tier. All three numbers are tunable defaults, flagged for revisiting once the still-pending scalability audit (`casewhy-scalability-capacity-audit-task.md`) reports real quota headroom.

Marketing copy reframes around "Unlimited case tracking for your family," with one honest, visible line noting tracking beyond typical household size may need a quick verification step — keeping the "unlimited" claim accurate rather than misleading once someone actually hits the gate. Any wording that needs to land in `terms.html`'s existing CaseWhy Plus billing section stays gated on Peter's standing ToS sign-off, same as every other Terms change on this project — this round is content/product copy only. Full task doc: `round46-unlimited-case-tracking-gated-task.md` (this repo, mirrored from the Ideas project).

**DONE Sep 9 (Claude Code).** `tracked_cases` gained a `status` column (`active` | `pending_review`) and `subscriptions` gained `effectiveMaxCases` (null = use the 10-case auto-approved band, set to 25 on approval) and `pendingApprovalToken`. The cron job now selects only `status = 'active'` rows — the actual quota-protection mechanism, not just a UI label. `trackCase()` computes the band on every insert: under `effectiveMaxCases` → `active`; at/above it but under the 25-case hard ceiling (`PLUS_HARD_CEILING_MAX_CASES` in `tier.ts`) → `pending_review`, firing the admin-notification email exactly once per crossing (checked by whether any `pending_review` row already existed before the insert, not per-case); at 25 → a hard-blocked error routing to `hello@casewhy.com`. Free tier is completely unaffected — bands are Plus-only.

**The "reuse existing magic-link token infrastructure" instruction was interpreted as reusing the *pattern*, not literally invoking Neon Auth's own magic-link generator** — that mechanism authenticates a specific user's sign-in session, not an unrelated admin batch-approval action, and isn't safely reusable that way. Built instead: a single-use `crypto.randomUUID()` token stored on the `subscriptions` row, a public-but-unguessable `GET /api/admin/approve-cases?token=` route (no new auth system, no new session type) that sets `effectiveMaxCases = 25` and flips **every** `pending_review` row for that account to `active` — not just the row that triggered the original notification, per the task's explicit requirement — then clears the token so the link can't be reused.

**A pending case is never given a live USCIS lookup either, not just excluded from the cron** — the dashboard and `/ask` both skip `getCaseStatus()` entirely for a `pending_review` receipt (a new `PendingReviewCard` renders instead), and `checkCaseNow()` rejects a pending case server-side as defense-in-depth even though the UI never exposes the button for one. `CaseSwitcher` shows pending cases with a distinct dashed amber pill + "Pending" label rather than hiding them.

`/plus`'s tracked-cases row now reads "Unlimited" with an honest one-line note about the review step for accounts past 10 cases. **Static-site copy sweep found one real stale reference** — `casewhy.com`'s own "CaseWhy Plus, coming at launch" card still said "Track up to 10 cases," fixed on `main` in the same pass. **Verified live end-to-end on a real Plus test account** (signed up fresh, seeded to exactly 10 active cases, since Peter's own account is real production traffic and wasn't touched): the 11th case, tracked through the real UI form, landed as `pending_review` and stayed there through two full page reloads — no live USCIS lookup, correctly excluded from a direct query of the cron's own poll list; the admin email fired exactly once (confirmed in Postmark's own outbound log, `Status: Sent`) despite the account later being pushed to 24 cases before approval, not once per case; the real approve link flipped `effectiveMaxCases` to 25 and all pending cases to active in one shot, and was confirmed single-use on a second visit; a case added right at 25 landed `active` directly (no more review needed); a 26th-case attempt hit the real hard-ceiling error with the "contact us" message. `tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds, deployed and confirmed live; all test data cleaned up after.

## Round 48, Sep 9 (cloud session) — Google search + Gemini links on every Get Help detail page

Peter's ask, discussed directly: give users a real, one-click way to do their own independent research on a listing, backing up the round-39 disclaimer and the ToS's "confirm directly, or through your own research" language with an actual mechanism. Two links, one line, one at each end — **detail/permalink pages only, never the list pages** — across all entity types (attorneys, accredited representatives, legal aid orgs live now; DSOs/community orgs once round 43 ships): "Search [Name] on Google" and "Ask Gemini about [Name]."

**Deliberately labeled differently, not presented as equivalent:** Google search is the real verification tool — actual web results a user can independently judge (bar-association profiles, reviews, the org's own site). Gemini is a general-info assistant, not verification — an LLM's synthesized answer carries real hallucination risk on exactly the question that matters most here (is this person actually licensed, is this org still operating), so it's labeled as asking a question, never as checking or verifying anything. Both links build their query from fields already shown on that same page (name, org/firm, city, state) — nothing fabricated, nothing sensitive, since directory data is public by design. Google link is a plain search URL; the Gemini link needs its actual current deep-link/prefill format confirmed before building rather than guessed at, falling back to a plain (non-prefilled) Gemini link if no reliable format is confirmed. No new tables, no click tracking, no ToS change needed. Full task doc: `round48-verification-links-task.md` (this repo, mirrored from the Ideas project).

**DONE Sep 9 (Claude Code).** Shared `<VerificationLinks>` component (`src/components/VerificationLinks.tsx`), wired into all five live entity-type permalink pages, confirmed absent from every list page via a direct grep of the live HTML. **Gemini's prefill format could not be safely confirmed live** — testing `gemini.google.com/app?prompt=...` hit a third-party ad-injecting browser-extension overlay (an unrelated "AITOPIA" upsell, not the real Gemini UI) sitting on top of a real, signed-in personal Google account in this environment; continuing to interact with it wasn't a safe or clean way to verify a URL parameter. Shipped the task's own explicit fallback instead: a plain `https://gemini.google.com/app` link, no guessed query parameter. **Worth flagging to Peter directly, separate from CaseWhy itself:** that ad-injector extension being active on a real signed-in Google account is worth knowing about. Google search link verified for real — spot-checked on a live attorney permalink, returned the exact right result (the State Bar of Texas's own "Find A Lawyer" page for that attorney) on the first hit. `tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds, deployed and confirmed live.

## Round 50, Sep 9 (cloud session) — table-driven CaseWhy Plus pricing, quarterly/annual plans

Peter's follow-up on the tiered-pricing idea he'd previously said not to build yet: go ahead and build it, but make the pricing itself table-driven rather than hardcoded, so future price changes don't need a code deploy. Written up as a design doc first (`casewhy-table-driven-pricing-concept.md`) and reviewed before anything was sent here, per Peter's explicit "write it up and let me review it first."

**Final decisions:** (1) a base-price change applies to new signups only — existing subscribers keep their original price, no migration; (2) seasonal promos, coupons, and referral discounts run entirely on Stripe's native Coupons + Promotion Codes, not a custom CaseWhy build; (3) no refund or proration policy at all for early cancellation on the new quarterly/annual plans — needs a plain, visible disclosure on `/plus` before purchase (checkout-page copy, not a `terms.html` change — that stays on the separate ToS sign-off track); (4) final prices: monthly $9.99 (unchanged), quarterly (3-month) $22.99 (~$7.66/mo), annual (12-month) $66.99 (~$5.58/mo).

**Revised same day: no admin page.** After seeing the full spec, Peter's call was that exposing a web page for this was over-engineered — he just wants future price changes to skip a big code change, not a UI to manage. The `plan_prices`/`pricing_rules` schema stays (that's the actual mechanism), but instead of `/admin/pricing`, a price change is made by running a small CLI script that inserts/deactivates a `pricing_rules` row, with the overlap guard enforced in the script rather than in a form.

**Build, in order:** Part 1 — the `plan_prices` + `pricing_rules` table (adjustments by fixed dollar amount or percent, over an effective-start/effective-end date range), the quarterly/annual Stripe Price setup, updated checkout flow, and the pricing-rule CLI script (no web page). Part 2 (after Part 1 ships) — turn on `allow_promotion_codes` in Checkout so Stripe's own dashboard-managed Coupons/Promotion Codes work at checkout; no new schema or UI for this piece either. Full task doc: `round50-table-driven-pricing-task.md` (this repo, mirrored from the Ideas project).

**DONE Sep 9 (Claude Code), both parts, same commit.** Verified live before building further: the current Stripe SDK (v22.6.1) does support inline `price_data` (with a `recurring` block) on a subscription-mode Checkout line item — confirmed by reading the actual type definitions, not assumed — so checkout computes the effective price server-side and passes it inline. No per-price-point Stripe Price objects to create or manage; a price change is purely a `pricing_rules` database row. `plan_prices` seeded with the three decided plans directly in the migration (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, no separate seed script needed for 3 fixed rows). `scripts/set-pricing-rule.ts` has `add`/`list`/`deactivate` modes; `add`'s overlap guard checks for any other active rule on the same plan with an overlapping date range and refuses rather than silently double-applying.

Verified end-to-end against real production (test-mode Stripe): the three plan cards and the "no refunds, no proration" disclosure render correctly on `/plus`; a real Stripe Checkout Session for the quarterly plan correctly showed "$22.99 every 3 months"; created a real `pricing_rules` test row ($2 off monthly) via the script, confirmed the live page picked it up ($9.99 → $7.99, rule label shown) and a fresh Stripe Checkout Session reflected the discounted $7.99 — that same checkout screen also confirmed Part 2's promo-code field is live ("Add promotion code"); confirmed the overlap guard refused a second conflicting rule on the same plan/date range; deactivated the test rule afterward and confirmed the price reverted to $9.99 live. Existing Stripe subscriptions are untouched by any of this by construction — the webhook handler never reads `plan_prices`/`pricing_rules`, only `startCheckout` does. `tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds, migration applied to production. Full detail in "Round 50" above.

## What this is

CaseWhy is an AI-explained USCIS case-status tracking web app. Existing trackers (Lawfully, US Case Tracker, VisaWatch) poll USCIS and show status. CaseWhy's differentiator: turn a status change or policy shift into a plain-language explanation and a concrete next action, instead of just a timestamp. **Updated Sep 5, 2026 (later that day):** the free/paid split described here is under active revision — see the new section below, "Scope expansion from Peter, Sep 5." The "ask a question" AI chat is no longer v2 — it's initial-rollout scope now, alongside a knowledge base, processing-time data, and visa bulletin tracking. Secondary revenue: attorney-referral warm handoff (still stretch).

## Origin

Peter spent 2026 exploring AI-leveraged business ideas he could build and run himself (15 years as a Solution Architect, wants to use AI for dev/marketing/ ops, B2C not B2B, $500-5,000 budget). CaseWhy was selected from a B2C shortlist on Sep 2, 2026. The idea is grounded in his own family's ordeal: N-400 naturalization applications for himself, his wife, and his daughter hit a 120+ day unexplained post-interview delay tied to a since-struck-down USCIS policy memo pausing naturalization for applicants born in ~39 "high-risk" countries — resolved only through a congressional inquiry and persistent follow-up. The product systematizes the plain-language status-tracking Peter built for his own case by hand.

Full background:

- Idea shortlist: "Ideas" Claude Project → `top-3-ai-business-ideas.md`, `top-3-b2c-app-ideas.md`
- Full MVP scope (architecture, legal risks, monetization, go-to-market): "Ideas" Claude Project → `immigration-case-companion-mvp-scope.md` — this is the source of truth for product/business decisions; this file is the source of truth for build status. **Correction Sep 4, 2026:** that doc is kept current by the cloud Claude session, not Claude Code — Claude Code doesn't have access to claude.ai Projects, so don't try to update it from here; report status back to this file (as always) and the cloud session will fold it in.

Key lives in a new `ENCRYPTION_KEY` env var (`.env.local`, Vercel Production/Preview, GitHub Actions secret — same three-places pattern as the other secrets). Verified in production: raw Postgres value is 56-byte ciphertext with no plaintext receipt number present, and decrypt round-trips correctly through the dashboard.

- **Email notification infrastructure: DONE and live end-to-end, Sep 5, 2026 (~1:40am ET).** `tracked_cases` extended with encrypted `email` (captured from `session.user.email` at track-time) and `lastStatusText`, plus plain `lastCheckedAt`. New `POST /api/cron/check-status` (`src/app/api/cron/check-status/route.ts`), protected by `Authorization: Bearer <CRON_SECRET>`: for each tracked case, calls the existing `getCaseStatus()`, compares to the last-seen status, and on a change sends via a new Postmark HTTP wrapper (`src/lib/email/postmark.ts`, plain `fetch`, no SDK) — but never on the *first* check after tracking (the user already saw that status when they tracked it; a notification then would be redundant). Switching a tracked case to a different receipt number resets the notification baseline, so a stale status from the old case can't fire a spurious email for the new one.
  - `sendStatusChangeEmail()` no-ops with a console warning if `POSTMARK_API_TOKEN` isn't set — this is deliberate, not a bug, and is why the polling/diff logic could be fully built and verified tonight without a real Postmark account existing yet. Verified locally end-to-end: first cron call sets a baseline (0 notifications), manually mutating the stored status and calling again correctly detects the change and attempts a send (`notified: 1`, no-op warning logged as expected). Verified in production too: the Bearer-auth guard rejects requests with no/wrong token (`401`) and accepts the right one (`200`, `{checked:0, notified:0, errors:[]}` since production has no tracked cases right now).
  - ~~Cron scheduling~~ — **done Sep 5, 2026, past midnight.** New dedicated cron-job.org account signed up under `info@casewhy.com` (not the NVDA trading project's — that one's at its 7-job free-tier cap; separate account keeps CaseWhy's infra cleanly apart). Test-fired from cron-job.org's own UI before saving — `200 OK`, correct `{"checked":0,"notified":0,"errors":[]}` body, first try, no repeat of the NVDA project's "missing literal `Bearer ` prefix" bug. Job is enabled, next run confirmed showing in the console.
  - ~~Postmark account + domain verification~~ — **done Sep 5, 2026, ~1:40am ET.** The user already had a Postmark free trial with `casewhy.com` + `info@casewhy.com` pre-added as a sender signature — just needed DKIM/Return-Path DNS verification, which Claude Code added directly at Cloudflare (both records resolving correctly via public DNS within seconds; Postmark's own "Verify" button confirmed both **Verified** on the first retry). Server API Token pulled from the account and added as `POSTMARK_API_TOKEN` (`.env.local`, Vercel Production/Preview, GitHub Actions secret). **Note: account is in Postmark's test mode** (100-email cap, can only send to `casewhy.com` addresses — fine for internal testing, but real users with e.g. `@gmail.com` addresses won't receive anything until "Request approval" is submitted and granted; that's a user-initiated step, not done yet).

Root cause, confirmed directly via the Vercel API (`GET /v9/projects/casewhy-app`): the project's actual Git integration has **`productionBranch: "main"`**, not `nextjs-app` — `main` on this repo is an effectively-unused branch (the real static landing page at casewhy.com is a *separate* Vercel project, "casewhy", not this one). The earlier "vercel inspect shows target: production" evidence was real but misleading — it was almost certainly from an earlier manual `vercel --prod` deploy, which forces `target: production` on that one deployment regardless of the Git branch setting, not from the branch setting actually being correct. **Fixed, Sep 5, 2026 ~5:00am ET.** The API/CLI attempts above were rejected (not a settable field there), but it turned out not to be a Git-tab setting at all in the current Vercel UI — it's under **Project Settings → Environments → Production → Branch Tracking**, changed there via the dashboard (Claude in Chrome) from `main` to `nextjs-app`, saved, then redeployed via the same page's "Redeploy" action to confirm: the new deployment built with `target: production` automatically and the `casewhy-app.vercel.app` alias updated correctly (`vercel inspect` confirmed, all four routes re-checked with real `curl` 200s). **This is genuinely resolved now** — every future push to `nextjs-app` should deploy straight to production on its own; no more manual `vercel promote` needed. If it ever regresses, check Project Settings → Environments → Production first, not the Git tab (that page only shows the GitHub App connection, not branch tracking).

The stable URLs, once a deployment actually is Production:

- **https://casewhy-app.vercel.app** — stable alias, click this one
- https://casewhy-app-smarticos.vercel.app — same deployment, alternate alias
- https://casewhy-app-git-nextjs-app-smarticos.vercel.app — branch-specific alias, will always point at the latest `nextjs-app` push

This is separate from casewhy.com, which is still the `main`-branch static landing page on a different Vercel project — no change there.

A real case lookup on that URL initially failed with "Something went wrong looking up your case" — the deployed app had no `USCIS_*` env vars set on Vercel at all. Fixed by adding them via `vercel env add` (had to strip stray double-quotes that `.env.local`'s formatting carries but Vercel doesn't). **Verified working end-to-end in-browser**, and now also verified again after the Sep 4 late-evening persistent-tracking/auth work below — see that section for what's new.

## New priorities from Peter, Sep 4 night (after reviewing live screenshots)

Two concrete build asks — **both done Sep 5, 2026, ~2:30am ET** — plus context on two questions that are a scope decision, not a build task yet:

1. ~~**Visual polish pass on sign-in + dashboard.**~~ — **Done.** Direct feedback was "the black background is dull," wanting a real design pass, not a quick color swap. Replaced the flat near-black dark background with a layered token system (`background`/`surface`/`surface-2`/`border`/`muted` CSS variables, swapped per `prefers-color-scheme`, wired into Tailwind so components don't need `dark:` pairs anymore) plus a subtle fixed brand-colored radial glow behind the page so dark mode reads as designed rather than flat. Added real typography (`next/font/google` Inter + JetBrains Mono, previously no font was configured at all — system default). Rebuilt the header into a sticky, bordered, backdrop-blurred nav with a real wordmark/logo mark (`src/components/Logo.tsx`); redesigned the dashboard's status card with a semantic color-coded status pill (green/red/amber/blue by keyword-matching the status text), a restyled "What this means" callout (icon + left accent bar instead of a plain tinted box), a real connecting-line history timeline, and a proper dropzone-style file upload control; redesigned the sign-in page to match (icon, card, success state with a checkmark). Verified in-browser via `npm run dev` + Claude in Chrome against the real sandbox case `EAC9999103403` (the same I-130-approved example from Peter's screenshot) in dark mode; `tsc --noEmit` and `npm run lint` both clean. Not yet re-verified against a real deploy or in light mode by eye — worth a look next time either comes up.
2. ~~**Sharpen the explanation layer's actual value-add.**~~ — **Done, tighter prompt, no knowledge base built yet.** Direct feedback was "where is the value-add of the explanation" — the I-130-approved example read as generic paraphrase-plus-boilerplate ("keep your notice safe," "consult an attorney if unsure"), not clearly differentiated from USCIS's own status text. Per the doc's own suggestion, tried the tighter-prompt route before concluding the curated policy/case-law knowledge base is required: `explainCaseStatus()` (`src/lib/ai/explain.ts`) now also feeds the model the case's **full status history** (every `hist_case_status` entry, not just the current one), **filed/last-updated dates with elapsed-days computed in code** (not left to the model's own date math), and a **receipt-number-derived processing center** (a small prefix→center table sourced from USCIS's own published receipt-number convention, e.g. `EAC` → Vermont Service Center; unrecognized prefixes are simply omitted rather than guessed). System instructions were also tightened to explicitly forbid generic-filler next steps unless the given facts actually support them. **Verified live against the same I-130 example**: the explanation now correctly references the case's second history entry (forwarded to a specific U.S. Embassy/Consulate) and gives a next step tied to that ("contact the U.S. Embassy or Consulate identified in your notice") instead of the old generic boilerplate — a real, visible improvement, not just a prompt-wording tweak. **Open question, not yet decided:** whether this is enough, or whether the curated knowledge base (grounding *why* a case is delayed against a known policy memo/backlog/court ruling — the thing a tighter prompt can't produce, since that's not in USCIS's own status text at all) is still worth prioritizing. Worth showing Peter this version before deciding.

Not build tasks yet — flagged so you're not blindsided if these come up in conversation:

- **"Where is the paywall / multiple receipts / free stuff to match competition"** — this came back with an answer, see the new section immediately below. **Still not decided:** the actual free-vs-paid packaging (Section 6 of the MVP doc is reopened, not resolved) — don't build multi-case gating or any paywall on your own initiative, since it affects the DB schema (`tracked_cases` currently enforces one row per account) and the roadmap's monetization sequencing.

## Scope expansion from Peter, Sep 5, 2026 (later that day) — four new build items

The competitor research came back (Lawfully, VisaWatch, and Case Tracker for USCIS & NVC — see the cloud session's "CaseWhy Competitive Comparison" artifact) and confirmed Peter's instinct: those three already give away free what CaseWhy was treating as paid-tier hooks or v2 stretch. Rather than just match the free tier, **Peter decided to build four features into initial rollout** so CaseWhy launches ahead of the competition on features, not just even with it. Added to the checklist as CW-31 through CW-34, in the order they probably need to happen. **Status as of Sep 5, ~4:15am ET: CW-31, CW-33(a), and CW-34 done; CW-32 deliberately not started.**

1. ~~**CW-31 — Curated policy/case-law knowledge base.**~~ — **Done, v1 scope: 3 entries, all sourced from primary/official documents, real (verified via live web research, not written from memory).** `src/lib/kb/policy-memos.ts`: (a) **PM-602-0194**, the January 1, 2026 hold on benefit applications from 39 "high-risk" countries — this is literally the memo Peter's own N-400 case was affected by (see Origin above) — including that it was later vacated/blocked by federal courts in MA/RI/TX through mid-2026; (b) the **2026 public charge rule** (DHS rescinded the 2022 rule, effective Sept 18, 2026, date-gated so it only surfaces for I-485s filed on/after that date); (c) the **RFE/NOID adjudicative-standard history** (2018 discretion-to-deny-without-RFE policy → rescinded 2021 → reverted to the 2013 "issue an RFE if more evidence could help" standard, which is still current). Each entry matched against a case **deterministically** (form type + keyword overlap with the case's own status text/history, or a filing-date cutoff for date-gated rules — see `findRelevantPolicyContext()`), capped at 2 matches — **never the model's own judgment**, so citations shown to the user can't be hallucinated. Wired into `explainCaseStatus()` (`src/lib/ai/explain.ts`) as "possibly relevant policy background," with explicit system-instruction guardrails to *never* present a match as confirmed fact (the Case Status API has no nationality field, so CaseWhy can never actually confirm e.g. the high-risk hold applies to a specific case — only that it plausibly might). Also now rendered as visible citations under the explanation on the dashboard (title + source link), not just folded silently into prose — this is the part that makes the value-add visible, not just felt. **Verified two ways:** a standalone unit-style check of the matching logic (5 synthetic cases, correct matches/non-matches including the real production baseline case matching nothing, as expected), and a real end-to-end call to `explainCaseStatus()` with a synthetic long-delayed N-400 — the model correctly cited the high-risk-hold memo, hedged appropriately ("may have been subject to..."), and suggested checking USCIS's real Emma chatbot. **Real estimate delivered as asked: this v1 took about half a day** (mostly research/sourcing, the code itself is small). Growing the KB further (more memos, deeper case-law) is real, ongoing editorial work — no fixed estimate for "comprehensive," and per the ticket's own framing this v1 (a handful of the most consequential items) was the right bar, not a first slice of a bigger planned build.
2. ~~**CW-32 — "Ask a question" AI chat.**~~ — **Done, Sep 5 ~6:30am ET.** Picked back up as the next real build item once CW-31 had a usable first version. Real chat, not a stub: `/ask` (signed-in + tracked-case-required, same gating pattern as the dashboard) renders `CaseChat` (`src/app/ask/CaseChat.tsx`), a client component posting to `POST /api/chat` (`src/app/api/chat/route.ts`). The route re-derives the user's tracked receipt number and re-fetches case status server-side from the authenticated session every request — it never trusts a client-supplied case status, only the conversation itself.
   - **Shared fact-gathering, refactored out first:** `explainCaseStatus()`'s prompt-building (form type, processing center, dates, history, matched policy background) was identical to what the chat needed, so it's now `buildCaseContext()` in the new `src/lib/ai/case-context.ts`, used by both `explain.ts` and the new `chat.ts` — real duplication removed, not just avoided going in.
   - **Guardrails, tightened beyond the explanation layer as the ticket asked for:** `src/lib/ai/chat.ts`'s system instructions add explicit handling for prediction requests ("will I be approved" → decline, point to `/processing-times` and `/visa-bulletin`, note USCIS is the only real source), strategic/legal questions (general concept if one exists, then an explicit "get an attorney" — not just a refusal), and staying on-topic. Never claims CaseWhy affiliation with USCIS/DHS.
   - **Verified against real adversarial questions, not just happy-path, per the ticket's own requirement** — ran 5 real questions through the live model against a synthetic long-delayed N-400: a percentage-approval-odds request (correctly declined, redirected to reference pages), "should I pay the officer to speed this up" (correctly refused, redirected to legitimate channels — congressional caseworker, attorney), a fraud/nationality-hiding question (correctly refused to advise, strongly redirected to an attorney, even surfaced free/low-cost legal-aid orgs), an off-topic question (politely redirected on-topic), and a legitimate "why is this stuck" question (grounded, specific, correctly hedged the matched policy-KB citation as background, not fact). All five passed cleanly on the first real run — see the "Real bug caught mid-testing" note below for the one thing that *didn't* pass cleanly at first.
   - **Real bug caught mid-testing, fixed before calling this done:** `relatedPolicies` is deterministic per-*case* (not per-question — see `findRelevantPolicyContext()`), so the first implementation attached the same policy citation to every assistant reply, including the off-topic "pizza toppings" test question — made it look like the citation was the reason for an unrelated answer. Fixed by moving it out of per-message display into a single persistent "Policy background that may apply to this case" panel shown once, above the conversation, updated from the latest response rather than repeated per bubble.
   - **Verification:** `tsc --noEmit` and `npm run lint` clean (same pre-existing `Logo.tsx` warning, unrelated). The full signed-in-but-no-tracked-case empty state was verified in-browser; the live tracked-case → chat round trip could *not* be verified end-to-end in-browser this pass — the USCIS sandbox was outside its published 7AM–8PM ET operating hours at the time (a real, time-of-day constraint hit before in this session, not a code issue). Confirmed instead against `casewhy-app.vercel.app` directly: unauthenticated `POST /api/chat` correctly returns `401`. (One transient false alarm on the way there, worth remembering: right after pushing, the same request briefly 404'd — a normal deploy-propagation timing gap, not a real bug — waited for `vercel ls` to show the new deployment `Ready` before retesting, then it was a clean `401`. Don't read a 404 immediately after a push as broken without re-checking against the deployment that's actually finished building.) The route reuses the exact same auth/tracked-case/`getCaseStatus()` pattern already proven working in the dashboard. **Worth a real click-through next session during sandbox hours before treating the full chat flow as verified in production.**
3. **CW-33 — Processing-time / approval-trend analytics.** ~~(a) surfacing USCIS's own published processing-time ranges~~ — **done**, see below. (b) Lawfully-style comparative analytics against CaseWhy's own accumulated case data — still correctly deferred, `tracked_cases` doesn't have enough real users yet for this to mean anything.
   - **Real, load-bearing finding before the "done": `egov.uscis.gov/processing-times` sits behind a Cloudflare bot challenge that blocks a plain fetch** — tried with a realistic browser User-Agent and got Cloudflare's "Attention Required" page, not the tool. This was assumed to be "a bounded, well-understood problem" in the original ticket; it isn't, if the plan was a live scraper — a Vercel serverless function would very likely get the same block. Loading the real tool in an actual browser (Claude in Chrome) passes the challenge fine, which is how the data below was actually captured.
   - **Built `src/lib/kb/processing-times.ts` + `/processing-times` page** with 4 real, precisely-labeled figures pulled live from the official tool (as of Sep 4, 2026): I-130 Immediate Relative (SCOPS) 24 months; I-140 EB-2 (SCOPS) 2.5 months; I-485 employment-based (SCOPS) 40 months — **important caveat surfaced directly on the page**, this SCOPS figure is the tool's own note that it only covers EB-4/EB-5, not EB-1/2/3 which are field-office adjudicated with no national aggregate; I-765 pending-I-485 (National Benefits Center) 11 months. N-400 and family-based I-485 are honestly called out as "office-specific, no national figure" (every N-400 is adjudicated by the applicant's own local field office — nothing in the Case Status API says which one) rather than either omitted silently or given a made-up number.
   - **Also surfaced, worth knowing**: the tool's own on-page alert says USCIS is mid-transition away from naming a specific service center (e.g. "Vermont Service Center") toward a consolidated "Service Center Operations (SCOPS)" bucket for processing-time *reporting* purposes. This doesn't break `serviceCenter()` in `explain.ts` (receipt-number prefixes still map to the historical centers, and correspondence still references them), but it's a real, current caveat if that function ever needs to line up with official processing-time data by center.
4. ~~**CW-34 — Visa bulletin tracking.**~~ — **Done, manual-update approach chosen** (per the same Cloudflare finding above — `travel.state.gov` is also behind the same bot challenge, confirmed by testing; a live scraper isn't realistic here either, so the "lighter alternative" the ticket offered is what got built). `src/lib/kb/visa-bulletin.ts` + `/visa-bulletin` page: real **Final Action Dates** for September 2026, both Family-Sponsored (F1–F4) and Employment-Based (EB-1–EB-3), by country (All Other/China/India/Mexico/Philippines) — captured by loading the real bulletin in a browser and reading the actual tables, not estimated or aggregated from secondary sources. Refresh cadence: monthly, by hand (or a Claude session with browser tools) — there's no API and no reliable way to automate the fetch, so this needs to become a recurring small task, not a one-time build.

**Please give a real estimate for these four once you've looked at them directly** rather than letting the cloud session's rough placeholder dates on the roadmap stand — the knowledge base especially could be days or could be weeks depending how deep Peter wants the initial version. The roadmap ("CaseWhy Launch Roadmap" artifact) has a first-pass guess (Sep 8–29) with the paid-launch milestone nudged from Nov 13 to a provisional Nov 17; treat both as placeholders pending your estimate, not commitments.

**Navigation gap, flagged Sep 5 (later):** today's dashboard is a single page with no menu at all — there's no way to reach four new pages once they exist. The cloud session added a rough sketch of a fix (a left-nav sidebar: Dashboard / Ask a question / Processing times / Visa bulletin) to the "CaseWhy Screens" artifact (https://claude.ai/code/artifact/35f8924b-837f-427e-81ec-1d0069d1ecef, see "Not built yet — main app navigation"). Treat it as a sketch that makes the problem visible, not a spec to implement literally — sidebar vs. top tabs vs. something else, and how it collapses on a phone width, are still open. Fold this into CW-28 (the queued visual-design pass) rather than building nav as separate throwaway work, since CW-28 was going to touch the same layout anyway.

## Marketing content in the app — recommendation on the Sep 5 port, and the cutover plan

Peter asked Claude Code directly to port the static site's "why CaseWhy" content into the app's own landing page, done Sep 5 ~5:35am ET (see `src/app/page.tsx` in "Key files" below — backlog-stats section, Track/Understand/Act grid, trust/disclaimer section, second email CTA, footer). He separately asked the cloud session for a recommendation on the same question before that landed. Below is that recommendation, **and items 1-3 are now done too, Sep 5 ~6:00am ET** — item 4 (tagline placement) is unchanged, still open, see "Branding decision" above:

1. ~~**The ported copy's core pitch is already a step behind the actual product.**~~ — **Done.** "What CaseWhy does differently" was a generic Track/Understand/Act 3-card grid; now 4 cards leading with what's actually built and real: "Understand, with real sources" (the CW-31 knowledge base's visible policy citations) and "See the full picture" (CW-33 processing times + CW-34 visa bulletin, right alongside the case status). Added a one-line subhead acknowledging plain-language explanations aren't a differentiator by themselves anymore (since competitors do that too) before naming what actually is.
2. ~~**Verify the backlog stats before treating them as the app's own permanent numbers.**~~ — **Done, both real and re-verified via live web research, not carried over unchecked.** "12M+ pending cases" → **12.1M+**, confirmed as USCIS's own total pending caseload, Q2 FY2026. "+42% backlog growth, year over year" → confirmed real but re-labeled **"net backlog growth"** specifically — the 42% figure is the *net* backlog (cases within USCIS's own control) growing from ~5M to ~7M between FY2025 Q2 and FY2026 Q2; a *different*, larger metric (total pending caseload) grew a separate 16.5% over a different year-over-year window (FY2025 Q1 → FY2026 Q1) in the same source data. The original static-site copy juxtaposed both numbers under generic "backlog growth" labels as if they were the same measure — they aren't, so this was a real, worthwhile catch, not just a rubber-stamp re-check. Added a one-line source/date attribution under the stat row, same spirit as CW-31's citations.

## Monetization ideas — prep for Claude Code, Sep 5 (evening) — scope and estimate, don't build the paywall yet

Peter wants these in front of you now, same pattern as CW-31–34: scope each one and give a real effort estimate before anything gets built. **This is explicitly NOT a green light to build a live paywall or take real payments** — there's no Florida LLC filed yet, no business bank account, no Stripe (or any payment processor) account set up, and the ToS/disclaimer language still needs the lawyer review budgeted in the MVP doc (~$500-1,500, not spent yet). What IS fair game right now: research, schema design, and any plumbing that doesn't require real billing to exist (e.g., a `subscription_tier` column that defaults to `"free"` for everyone, a usage-counter table). Don't wire actual tier-gating logic live yet — the free-vs-paid packaging itself is still Peter's open call (MVP doc Section 6 is reopened, not resolved).

These came out of fresh competitive research (VisaWatch, Lawfully, Case Tracker for USCIS & NVC) the cloud session did Sep 5 evening — full writeup and reasoning in the "CaseWhy Competitive Comparison" artifact and the MVP doc Section 6 (Claude Code can't read the MVP doc directly since it lives in a claude.ai Project — everything you need is duplicated here). Ranked in the cloud session's recommended priority order:

**Pricing/packaging decision — confirmed by Peter, Sep 5 (evening), answering your CW-35/CW-36 questions directly:**

- **One bundled paid tier, not separate add-ons.** Working name "CaseWhy Plus." A single subscription unlocks unlimited AI chat (CW-35) *and* multi-case tracking (CW-36) together, plus whatever else from this list ships later (CW-37/38/40) — not a menu of separate a-la-carte purchases. Matches how all three competitors structure their own paid tiers, and is simpler for a solo founder to build/support/market than multiple SKUs.
- **Price: $9.99/mo** for that one tier. Matches the general consumer-subscription-app market median (not immigration-specific, but the most defensible anchor found), sits mid-range in the original $7-15/mo target from the MVP doc, and is a familiar/expected price point.
- **CW-35 free-tier chat limit: 10 questions per month.** Unlimited on the $9.99/mo paid tier. Treat 10/month as a starting hypothesis, not a permanent number — worth revisiting once there's real usage data on how often people actually use the chat (a case's status doesn't change often, so most free users likely won't hit this most months — its real job is catching the people who lean on it heavily, who are also the most likely to convert).
- **CW-36 case cap: 5 tracked cases**, not unlimited, same $9.99/mo tier (no separate/higher price for this). Deliberately bounded rather than unlimited — mirrors Peter's own household (himself, wife, daughter, with room to grow) and prevents the plan from being valuable enough to informally resell/share across an unrelated group, which would undercut the whole per-subscription pricing model.
- **Still true, unchanged:** none of this is a green light to build a live paywall yet (see the no-LLC/no-Stripe/no-lawyer-review caveat above) — build the packaging logic, schema (`subscription_tier`, usage-counter table, the 5-case cap), and UI/upgrade-prompt copy now; wire it to real billing once the business prerequisites exist.

1. ~~**CW-35 — Meter the "ask a question" AI chat (CW-32), don't gate it entirely.**~~ — **Done, Sep 5 ~7:40am ET, schema/logic only (see the standing no-live-billing caveat above — real numbers now confirmed, but still no Stripe to actually charge anyone).** New `chat_usage` table (userId + year-month + count) via `src/lib/billing/chat-usage.ts`; `POST /api/chat` checks the month's count against the tier's limit (10/month free, unlimited plus) *before* calling `chatAboutCase()` — so a blocked request never costs an AI Gateway call — and returns a distinct `402` with `limitReached: true` when hit. `CaseChat.tsx` shows "X of 10 free questions left this month" after each reply and disables the input with an upgrade message once reached.
2. ~~**CW-36 — A family / multi-case plan.**~~ — **Done, Sep 5 ~7:40am ET — the flagged schema change, applied while `tracked_cases` was still empty.** Dropped the old `tracked_cases_user_id_idx` **unique** index (real migration `0002_messy_cyclops.sql`, applied directly against the live Neon DB via `drizzle-kit migrate`, verified after via a direct query that it's now a plain non-unique index) — `tracked_cases` is genuinely multi-row-per-account now, capped in application code (not the DB) at free=1/plus=5 via the new `src/lib/billing/tier.ts` (`subscriptions` table, no row = free — same "no live billing" caveat as CW-35). This required real, not-trivial UI work beyond the schema: a `CaseSwitcher` component (pill row, reuses the existing `?receipt=` query-param convention) now shared by both `/dashboard` and `/ask` when a user has more than one tracked case; `TrackCaseButton` now has three states (already-tracked-with-untrack, can-track, at-cap-with-upgrade-message) instead of the old binary one; a new `untrackCase()` action. **Verified two ways:** a direct script against the real DB (tier defaults to free with no row, tracked-case decrypt/order, tier flip to plus, chat-usage counting through to `limitReached`, full cleanup back to empty) — all passed exactly as designed; and a real in-browser click-through (seeded 2 temporary tracked cases for the actual signed-in test account, confirmed the switcher renders and correctly re-orders active/inactive pills on both pages, confirmed switching updates the URL and re-renders, then deleted the seed data and confirmed the dashboard is back to its clean empty state). **Confirmed, not just assumed:** the cron `check-status` job needed zero changes — it already iterates every `tracked_cases` row independently by `row.id`, never assumed one-row-per-user.
   - **Real bug caught before it shipped:** the original `TrackingLimitError` custom error class lived in `src/app/dashboard/actions.ts`, which crashed the whole app with a 500 the moment it was imported — Next.js's `"use server"` file convention only allows exported *async functions*, not classes. Fixed by throwing a plain `Error` with the same message instead; caught by testing `/dashboard` and `/ask` in-browser right after writing the code, not just trusting `tsc --noEmit` (which doesn't catch this — it's a Next.js build-time convention, not a type error).

3. **CW-37 — Faster / on-demand status checks.** Free keeps today's daily cron; paid gets hourly polling or a manual "check now" button. Before committing to hourly-for-all-paid-users, check the production USCIS API's actual rate/TPS ceiling (sandbox limits may not reflect production) — this could become a real constraint once there's paid volume.
   - **Estimate, Sep 5 evening — scoped, not built.** The rate-limit math already answers the "hourly for all paid users" question without needing new research: the onboarding spec (see artifact below) already records production's quota as ~10,000 lookups/day, 10 TPS (vs. sandbox's 1,000–3,500/day, 5 TPS). Hourly polling is 24 checks/day/user — at just ~416 paid users doing that, the *entire production daily quota* is gone before the daily cron for free users even runs. That's a real, near-term ceiling, not a hypothetical one. **Recommendation: build the manual "check now" button for v1** (small — a server action that calls `getCaseStatus()` on demand for one tracked case, updates `lastStatusText`/`lastCheckedAt` outside the cron cycle, gated to plan tier the same schema/logic-only way as CW-35/36), and treat automatic hourly-for-all-paid-users as a later milestone that needs real volume data before committing to it, not a v1 requirement. Estimate: ~1-2 hours for the button once picked up. Not started.

Commercial fit (if built): free standalone rep lookup, metered/paid AI-assisted letter drafting — same "meter the AI part" pattern as CW-35.

The doc's "for a paid consultation moment" framing suggests gating was the intent, but doesn't say so explicitly — flagging rather than assuming. Estimate: ~2-3 hours once picked up (add dependency, build the PDF layout, wire the route/button, decide the gating question). Not started.

## Next tasks for Claude Code — round 3, Sep 5 (evening) — CW-33 expansion: all forms + location lookup

Peter looked at the CW-33(a) processing-times page and wants it extended well beyond the current 4 hardcoded form+category entries: **all USCIS forms, including a form + location (city/office) lookup**, matching the structure of USCIS's own official tool. His words: "extend the function for all forms including the ones requiring location input, similar to what uscis offers by entering the form number and city to provide the processing time by form by center/city."

**Test this before scoping anything else — it could change the whole approach:** USCIS's actual processing-times tool is a client-side SPA that calls an internal JSON API, documented (via reverse-engineering) in the open-source `coatless-r-n-d/uscis-processing` R package as `https://egov.uscis.gov/processing-times/api/`. This is a *different surface* from `egov.uscis.gov/processing-times` itself — the HTML page you already confirmed is Cloudflare-blocked for CW-33(a)/CW-34. Nobody has tested whether this specific API endpoint carries the same block. Use Claude in Chrome to load the real tool, check `read_network_requests` for the exact calls it makes (URL, headers, payload shape), then try hitting that endpoint directly (plain fetch, realistic UA) the same way you tested the HTML page. Two very different outcomes hinge on this: if it's open, this becomes a real live comprehensive lookup instead of another hand-maintained table — a materially better result. If it's blocked the same way, the existing manual-capture pattern has to scale up a lot (see below), which is a much bigger, different-shaped effort. Don't scope the rest until you know which world this is.

**Location structure, so the picker design doesn't guess:** interview-based forms (N-400, family-based I-485 — the ones CW-33(a) already had to punt on as "office-specific, no national figure") are adjudicated by **92 field offices + 130 Application Support Centers**, keyed to the applicant's own specific office — this is the "city" input Peter's describing. Petition-based forms (I-129/I-130/I-140, etc.) instead go through exactly **5 service centers**: Vermont (990), California (991), Nebraska (992), Texas (993), Potomac (1031) — a much smaller, already-enumerable set, closer to what CW-33(a) already models with `serviceCenter()` in `explain.ts`. These are structurally different lookups (many field offices vs. 5 centers) and USCIS's own tool treats them differently — worth designing the form+location picker around that split rather than one generic dropdown for everything.

**Existing open-source references, unvetted but worth a look before building from scratch:** `gr2m/uscis-service-center-processing-times` (npm/JS package, service-center scope — most directly reusable given this is already a Next.js/TS app), `coatless-r-n-d/uscis-processing` (R package, documents the API endpoint above), `jzebedee/uscis` (daily scraper/dataset — flags that USCIS began "aggressively blocking scraping" as of Feb 2025 and now needs residential proxies, a real caution that even a currently-open API endpoint might not stay reachable), `hmakkapati/uscis-tracker`. None of these are vetted for license or reliability — leads, not endorsements.

**Scale:** USCIS's own tool covers several dozen form types, several with per-category variants — meaningfully more than today's 4 entries, though "several dozen" is an imprecise figure from research, not a count from the tool itself; get the real number once you're looking at whichever surface (API or manual) ends up being the actual data source.

**Ask, same pattern as CW-31/33(a)/34/37/40: test the API endpoint first, then give a real effort estimate for whichever path it points to** — don't build the full-scale version yet. The two possible outcomes (live API integration vs. a much larger hand-captured dataset plus an office/city picker UI) are different enough in shape and size that an estimate before testing the endpoint would just be a guess. Report back what the endpoint test shows either way, even if it's blocked — that's a real, useful finding on its own, same as the CW-33(a)/CW-34 Cloudflare findings were.

1. ~~**CW-33(c) — test the processing-times API endpoint**~~ — **Tested, Sep 5 ~9:15am ET. Real, conclusive "no" — this is a materially different finding than "Cloudflare-blocked like before," worth reading carefully before deciding how to proceed.**

That reference is stale; the current tool doesn't use it.
   - **What the current tool actually does, confirmed via `read_network_requests` while driving the real form:** selecting a form fires a same-origin `POST https://egov.uscis.gov/processing-times` (the *same* URL as the page itself, not a separate API path) that returns server-rendered HTML, not JSON. There is no separate, lower-friction API surface to test against — the only "endpoint" *is* the page, and it's behind the identical Cloudflare wall already established for CW-33(a)/CW-34. A plain `curl`/`fetch` from a serverless function would hit the same block a `curl` to the HTML page does; only a real, human-driven browser session gets through.
   - **This changes the shape of the ask, not just the estimate.** "All forms + any city/office" as literally scoped would mean manually querying the tool once per form+category+office combination — there's no bulk/export view in the tool itself. With ~40 form types (several with multiple categories) × 92 field offices + 130 ASCs for interview-based forms, that's not "a bigger hand-captured dataset," it's an unbounded number of individual browser-driven lookups with no realistic refresh cadence — a fundamentally different (and impractical) undertaking from CW-33(a)'s 4 entries or CW-34's one-page-per-month bulletin.

(b) Rather than hand-building a directory of the 92 field offices + 130 ASCs (real USCIS data that changes over time), `/processing-times` now links straight out to USCIS's own locator pages — confirmed live and correct in a real browser: `uscis.gov/about-us/find-a-uscis-office/field-offices` and `.../application-support-centers` (note: no "-ascs" suffix — a first guess at that URL 404'd, caught before shipping). ## Next tasks for Claude Code — round 5, Sep 5/6 (overnight) — fix the sign-in email's branding

Peter tried registering and got a real, unbranded sign-in email — worth fixing soon, it's a trust problem for exactly the audience CaseWhy is trying to reassure (an unfamiliar sender domain reads as suspicious to someone already anxious about their case, and this product's whole pitch is trustworthy information). The email: subject "Sign In to proud-wind-48334029", from `Neon Auth <auth@mail.myneon.app>` — both are Neon Auth defaults, not a bug, and both are documented as fixable:

1. **Quick fix — the project name in the subject/body.** Neon Console → Auth → Configuration tab → Project Info panel → **Application Name** field. Per Neon's own docs, this defaults to the raw Neon project slug (explaining "proud-wind-48334029") and is exactly what shows up in user-facing auth emails. Set it to "CaseWhy". Two-minute fix, no code change, do this first.
2. **Real fix — the sender domain.** Neon's docs (Auth production checklist) explicitly recommend replacing the shared `auth@mail.myneon.app` SMTP with a custom provider: *"Replace shared SMTP (`auth@mail.myneon.app`) with your own email service for reliable delivery and higher limits."* CaseWhy already has a fully verified Postmark sending domain for `casewhy.com` (DKIM + Return-Path done for CW-24's notification emails) — reuse that instead of setting up a new provider. Configure it as Neon Auth's custom SMTP provider in Auth production settings so sign-in emails come from something like `CaseWhy <auth@casewhy.com>` instead of Neon's domain.
3. **Optional, bigger lift, not urgent: full template control.** Neon supports intercepting `send.otp`/`send.magic_link` webhook events and sending a fully custom-branded HTML email (real logo, real design-system colors) through Postmark directly instead of Neon's default template — covered in Neon's "Customize emails" doc. Worth doing eventually so the sign-in email actually looks like CaseWhy, but steps 1-2 alone fix the actual trust problem Peter hit and should ship first.

**Ask:** do 1 and 2 now — both are config, not new code, and nothing blocks either. Report back once sender/subject are confirmed branded with a real test sign-in email. Scope 3 only if there's a natural opening; not worth interrupting other work for.

**Done, Sep 6 (Claude Code, via Neon Console — no code change, config only).** Both 1 and 2 are set: Application Name → "CaseWhy"; Email provider → Custom SMTP provider, `smtp.postmarkapp.com:587`, sender `auth@casewhy.com`, reusing the same Postmark Server API Token already in `POSTMARK_API_TOKEN`. **Verified two ways:** Neon's own "Send test email" button reported success against `info@casewhy.com` (proves the SMTP credentials actually authenticate with Postmark — a wrong token would have errored there, not succeeded); and the config page itself now shows Server: "Custom SMTP provider" and Sender address: `auth@casewhy.com` (was "Shared" / `auth@mail.myneon.app`). **Not independently confirmed by Claude Code:** the actual received email's subject/sender text, since Claude Code doesn't have inbox access to `info@casewhy.com` (same Gmail-delegation gap noted elsewhere in this file) — Peter, worth a quick look at the real inbox to confirm the subject reads "Sign In to CaseWhy" (not the old project slug) and the sender shows as CaseWhy. Scope 3 (fully custom HTML templates) still not done — untouched, per the original "not urgent" framing.

2. **Elevate round 6's "also check, don't assume" item to a firm build requirement, not just a check:** confirm whether the static `casewhy.com` site's own copy of this form shares anything with the app, then wire it to something real too — reuse the same backend if that's simpler given the two are separate deployments, or give it its own small table if not. Either way, **both forms need to actually capture an email in production**, not just the app's.
3. Skip the honeypot/rate-limiting hygiene item for now if it would delay shipping — already scoped as "not urgent enough to block."
4. **This is deliberately decoupled from round 7** (the logo/tagline/color-match/founder's-note/copy refresh below). Round 7 can land whenever it's ready — don't bundle it with this or let it hold up shipping the actual capture mechanism. Build and ship email capture first; round 7 follows after.
