## Next tasks for Claude Code — round 23, Sep 7 (cloud session) — I-589 (asylum) and I-821D (DACA), extended user base, extra care required

Peter confirmed extending coverage to these two as well, after the earlier flag that they carry materially higher stakes than everything added so far — asylum for individualized-fact/credibility sensitivity with real deportation consequences if guidance is wrong, DACA because its legal status is genuinely still moving through active litigation. Real research was done on both before drafting this (not assumed from general knowledge, since both are exactly the kind of thing that goes stale fast) — current as of this session, Sep 7, 2026, and **must be re-verified at actual build time**, not treated as still-accurate by default given how fast both have been moving.

**Before this ships to real users:** Peter should personally read through the actual KB content and the guardrail-test transcripts for both form types before go-live — not just "verified live in a browser" the way other rounds have closed out. The stakes here are categorically different from a UI reorder or a news-feed list.

**Peter's explicit decision, Sep 8 (cloud session), after seeing how the competition handles this (see the addendum above):** go all-in on both form types, matching Lawfully's choice rather than VisaWatch's avoidance of them — but with strict guardrails, erring on the side of safety wherever a question is close. This authorizes Claude Code to actually start the build (it hasn't yet — round 23 remains the one open round from Sep 7). "Strict, err on safety" means, concretely, on top of everything already specified in this section: when a user's question sits anywhere near the line between general process information and something case-specific or outcome-predictive, the guardrail should decline and route to an attorney rather than attempt a careful hedge — for these two form types specifically, prefer an over-cautious refusal over an under-cautious answer, a stricter bar than the "over-hedged rather than declining outright" issue caught and fixed on I-765 in round 21. The mandatory gate is unchanged: this round still can't be marked done until Peter has personally read the real KB content and guardrail-test transcripts, regardless of how clean the automated testing looks.

### 1. I-589 (Application for Asylum)

**One-year filing deadline.** Must generally file within one year of arrival in the US, with exceptions for changed circumstances materially affecting eligibility, or extraordinary circumstances that caused the filing delay. State this as a general rule with real exceptions, never as a flat "you missed it, you're out of luck" — the exceptions are real and case-specific, exactly the kind of thing that needs a real attorney, not an AI guess.

**Affirmative vs. defensive matters — a distinction CaseWhy's guardrails must get right.** Someone filing I-589 directly with a USCIS asylum office (affirmative) is on a completely different process and timeline than someone filing it as a defense in removal proceedings before an immigration judge (defensive, EOIR — not USCIS at all). Conflating these would be a real, embarrassing accuracy failure, not just an omission — the explanation logic needs to know (or explicitly not assume) which track a given case is on.

**Work authorization timing — currently unsettled, handle with real caution.** As of this research (Feb-Sep 2026), the standing rule is roughly a **150-day** wait after filing before applying for an initial EAD, per current guidance ([Fragomen](https://www.fragomen.com/insights/united-states-dhs-proposes-restrictions-on-employment-authorization-for-asylum-applicants.html)) — though other sources cite 180 days depending on which point in the process they're measuring from, so don't present a single number as certain. **A DHS rule proposed Feb 23, 2026** would extend this to 365 days, add a biometrics requirement, add new eligibility bars, and let USCIS pause new EAD applications entirely when asylum processing exceeds 180 days ([Federal Register](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/02/23/2026-03595/employment-authorization-reform-for-asylum-applicants)) — **as of this research it was still a proposed rule, not finalized** (comment period closed April 24, 2026; still needs to clear OMB review). **Check whether this rule has since finalized before writing the KB entry's final wording** — if it has, the entry needs the new numbers; if not, say clearly "currently X days, a change to 365 days has been proposed but not finalized as of [date]" rather than picking one number and asserting it as settled.

**EO 14161 relevance, already flagged elsewhere in this project:** this population is exactly who that social-media-vetting policy targets — a real reason to keep guidance strictly procedural/factual and never speculative about outcomes or risk.

### 2. I-821D (Consideration of Deferred Action for Childhood Arrivals)

**Current status, verified as of Aug 15, 2026 ([National Immigration Forum](https://forumtogether.org/article/current-status-of-daca-explainer/)):** USCIS is only processing **renewals** for current DACA recipients — it is not accepting or processing new initial applications at all. Any KB content or guardrail must reflect that a new applicant genuinely cannot get DACA right now, not soften that into ambiguity.

**Litigation status, actively unresolved:** the Fifth Circuit ruled January 17, 2025 in *Texas v. United States* that deferred action itself (deportation protection) is constitutional and can apply nationwide, but that DACA's work-permit component may be unlawful and severable from the rest of the program; the case was remanded to the district court (Judge Hanen) to issue a modified order, and **as of July 1, 2026 no modified order had been issued yet** — meaning the work-permit question is genuinely still open. The narrowed injunction from this ruling applies only to Texas. There were roughly **120,000 pending DACA renewal applications** as of this research — the highest number on record, creating real processing-delay risk of recipients lapsing between work authorization/protection periods through no fault of their own.

**This is the single most legally volatile form type in this entire expansion.** Recommend the KB entry explicitly state the date it was last verified, link out to a live-updating source for the current litigation status (e.g., FWD.us's DACA case tracker) rather than asserting a permanent fact, and that Claude Code build in a lightweight recurring reminder (a comment in the code, or a CLOUD_CLAUDE.md tracked item) to re-verify this specific entry periodically — not a one-time write that quietly goes stale while people rely on it for something with real deportation/work-authorization stakes.

**Guardrail, non-negotiable:** never state or imply that new DACA applications are currently possible, and never predict how the pending litigation will resolve or when.

### 3. Case-type dropdown, KB, and guardrail testing

Same mechanics as rounds 21-22 — add both to the dropdown, write the KB entries per the verified facts above (re-verify immediately before writing, given the pace of change on both), and run the same CW-32-style adversarial pass. For these two specifically, also test: a user asking the AI to predict their case outcome or asylum grant likelihood (must decline), and for DACA, a user asking if they can apply for the first time right now (must clearly say no, current renewals only, and why).

**Sequencing:** round 22 (I-129, I-751) has no special gating and can proceed on its own timeline. Round 23 (I-589, I-821D) should not be marked done until Peter has done the personal content review described above, even if the code itself is built, tested, and verified live in the meantime.

**Built and deployed live, Sep 8 (Claude Code) — round 25's Terms tightening shipped first, per its own sequencing requirement, before this round's dropdown went live.** New "Asylum & humanitarian" dropdown group (I-589, I-821D) added to `case-type-timeline.ts`; 2 new KB entries in `policy-memos.ts` using the exact content already reviewed by Peter in the round-23 review artifact (`https://claude.ai/code/artifact/624195e5-ddc5-495d-ba18-387b11e9d1bb`), facts re-verified fresh at build time and confirmed unchanged; explicit hard-rule guardrails added to `explain.ts`/`chat.ts` for every mandatory scenario, deliberately the strictest bar in the app — decline and redirect rather than hedge on anything close to the line.

**Adversarial-tested against the real backend, 12 questions across both form types, both mandatory scenarios included.** One real finding on the first pass: the I-589 deadline-exception test hedged with a conditional assessment ("could sometimes matter if...") instead of declining outright — short of the explicit "err on safety" bar. Fixed with two successive guardrail-tightening passes (the rule now forbids engaging with a user's own described facts at all, even conditionally, not just forbidding outcome predictions) and re-verified clean. All other 11 questions passed cleanly on the first run. Full real transcripts, the KB content, and the one finding-and-fix are all in the review artifact above, updated with the live transcripts.

**DONE — Peter personally reviewed the KB content and guardrail-test transcripts in the artifact and approved it ("it is a go"), Sep 8.** Round 23 is complete: built, adversarial-tested, deployed live, and now reviewed against its own explicit gate. Artifact updated to reflect the approval: `https://claude.ai/code/artifact/624195e5-ddc5-495d-ba18-387b11e9d1bb`.

**Addendum, Sep 8 (cloud session) — how the competition actually handles asylum and DACA, checked directly.** Peter asked how the three competitors already tracked in the "CaseWhy Competitive Comparison" artifact handle these two form types, since it bears on this round's decision. Checked each one's real App Store listing/site (Sep 8, 2026), not assumed:

- **Lawfully explicitly supports both**, branded by name — its App Store listing lists "I-821D (DACA), Asylum, DS-260 and NVC consular cases" directly alongside its other 20 supported forms, plus general EOIR/immigration-court case tracking. This is real precedent: a real, established competitor is already doing exactly what this round proposes, not something CaseWhy would be first to attempt.
- **VisaWatch supports neither** — its published form list (I-485, I-130, I-140, I-129, I-129F, N-400, I-765, I-131, I-90, plus EB/F category tracking) has no asylum or DACA category at all, consistent with its simpler, "zero lawyer banners" positioning. A real competitor choosing to stay out of this territory too, worth weighing alongside Lawfully's choice to go in.
- **Case Tracker for USCIS & NVC (ImmiVision)** — the specific competitor whose screenshots triggered this project's case-type-expansion rounds — doesn't brand or market asylum/DACA support specifically, but its tracking is generic receipt-prefix-based ("all valid USCIS case receipts... EAC, IOE, LIN, MCT, MGL, MSC, NBC, SRC, WAC, YSC, ZAR, ZCH, ZHN") plus NVC and EOIR cases, so it likely *can* track an I-589 or I-821D receipt number without treating it as a distinct, marketed feature.

The "CaseWhy Competitive Comparison" artifact's "Case types covered" row has been updated with this detail (`https://claude.ai/code/artifact/dfb67690-8235-4179-b4bb-def2e4748f54`). Not a build item — this is context for Peter's own pending content review above, not a reason to skip or shortcut it.

## Next tasks for Claude Code — round 24, Sep 8 (cloud session) — resource-hub links, a sign-out bug, and hyperlinking Visa Bulletin / N-400 / I-485 mentions

Peter's request came with 2 screenshots of a competitor app's "Resources" page, plus three unrelated items in the same message: a sign-out bug report, a multi-state sales-tax question (answered directly to Peter in chat, not a build item — see the note at the end of this round), and a request to hyperlink "Bulletin" and N-400/I-485 mentions somewhere in the app.

### 1. Add more curated links to the resources hub (`src/lib/resources/links.ts`)

Checked the live file first — the existing 7 (`case-status`, `processing-times`, `visa-bulletin`, `all-forms`, `field-office-locator`, `asc-locator`, `ombudsman`) already cover the case-status tool, processing times, the Visa Bulletin, the forms index, the field-office locator, the ASC locator, and the Ombudsman. Add these 7 new entries, none of which duplicate the above — same discipline as round 14: **live-verify every URL with a real browser-like fetch before shipping, same as the note already at the top of this file**, and drop or fix any that don't check out rather than shipping a guessed URL:

- **Civil Surgeon Locator** — `https://my.uscis.gov/findadoctor` — for finding a doctor authorized to do the medical exam (Form I-693), relevant to anyone on the green-card track.
- **AR-11, Change of Address** — `https://www.uscis.gov/addresschange` — required within 10 days of moving; a genuinely common miss that causes real problems (missed notices).
- **Adjustment of Status Filing Charts** — `https://www.uscis.gov/green-card/green-card-processes-and-procedures/adjustment-of-status-filing-charts-from-the-visa-bulletin` — the companion chart USCIS publishes alongside the Visa Bulletin telling filers which of the Bulletin's two tables (Final Action vs. Dates for Filing) currently applies; genuinely distinct from the existing `visa-bulletin` link, not a duplicate.
- **Case Inquiry (E-Request)** — `https://egov.uscis.gov/e-request/` — for when a case is outside normal processing times and the user wants to formally ask USCIS about it, rather than just watching the status.
- **CBP I-94 lookup** — `https://i94.cbp.dhs.gov/I94/#/home` — arrival/departure record, relevant to anyone whose case depends on their last entry date or current status.
- **Civics Test study materials** — `https://www.uscis.gov/citizenship/find-study-materials-and-resources` — official study resources for the N-400 civics/English test.
- **myUSCIS appointment scheduler** — `https://my.uscis.gov/en/appointment/v2` — confirmed this session as the current name/URL for what used to be branded "InfoPass"; label it plainly (e.g. "Schedule an in-person appointment") rather than using the retired "InfoPass" name, which could confuse a user searching for it.

**Deliberately not adding:** the competitor screenshot also showed something like a "Priority Date Estimator" — that's their own proprietary predictive feature, not an external government resource, so it doesn't belong in a curated links list. If CaseWhy wants something like it, that's a separate product-feature discussion, not a links-hub addition.

### 2. Bug: sign-out doesn't refresh the page

Reported directly by Peter. Found the likely cause in `src/components/AuthHeader.tsx`:

```
onClick={() => authClient.signOut().then(() => router.refresh())}
```

`router.refresh()` re-fetches the current route's server-rendered data, but it doesn't necessarily do two things a real sign-out needs: (a) force `authClient.useSession()` — the client-side hook driving the header's own signed-in/signed-out UI — to re-read the now-cleared session, and (b) move the user off a page that requires auth (e.g. `/dashboard`) once they're signed out. Depending on which of those is actually broken, the symptom could be "header still shows the old email," "stuck on a now-invalid protected page," or both.

Please investigate and fix for real rather than guessing — confirm which piece is actually failing (add a console log or step through it in a real browser session) before changing anything. Likely direction: redirect to `/` after sign-out (`router.push("/")`, or whatever the auth client's own sign-out options expose for a post-signout redirect) in addition to or instead of `router.refresh()`, and confirm whether `authClient.useSession()` updates reactively on its own once `signOut()` resolves (many auth client libraries' session stores are reactive without needing a manual refresh — if so, the missing piece is purely the redirect, not the session state). Test on both a public page and a protected app page, since the failure may look different on each. Verify live in a real signed-in browser session, not just a code read-through.

### 3. Hyperlink "Visa Bulletin" and N-400 / I-485 mentions

Peter wants Visa Bulletin references and N-400/I-485 form-name mentions turned into real links wherever they show up in the app. The existing `linkifyExplanation()` in `src/lib/kb/linkify.tsx` (round 20) only links terms that a case's own matched KB policies already surfaced (`findRelevantPolicyContext`) — it's deliberately narrow and won't fire for "Visa Bulletin," "N-400," or "I-485" unless those happen to already be matched keywords for that case. This needs a second, simpler always-on pass, not an extension of the existing narrowly-scoped one:

- A small new term-link table (e.g. `TERM_LINKS` in a new `src/lib/kb/term-links.ts`, same "hand-curated, not scraped" pattern as `RESOURCE_LINKS`), something like: `"Visa Bulletin"` → CaseWhy's own `/visa-bulletin` page (internal — keeps the user in-app and matches the SEO/engagement role that page and `/processing-times` already play, rather than sending them straight to the external Dept. of State site the way the resources-hub link does; this is a judgment call, revisit if it conflicts with anything), `"N-400"` → `https://www.uscis.gov/n-400`, `"I-485"` → `https://www.uscis.gov/i-485`.
- A new `linkifyTerms()` function (or a second pass folded into the existing render path) applied wherever AI-generated explanation prose is rendered — same component(s) that already call `linkifyExplanation()` — so both passes can run together without one clobbering the other's matches (watch for overlap the same way the existing function already guards against overlapping KB matches).
- Keep scope to the AI explanation prose for this round, the same tested hook point `linkifyExplanation` already uses. If it turns out to be easy and low-risk to also apply on `/news` article summaries or the KB/static pages, fine, but don't expand scope there if it adds real complexity — flag it as a possible follow-up instead.

**Not part of this round, informational only:** Peter's multi-state sales-tax question was answered directly in chat, not built into a task — quick summary for context: Florida doesn't tax SaaS (confirmed via a real Florida DOR Technical Assistance Advisement), roughly 25 other states do as of last check, and Stripe Tax automates the rate calculation but the business still owns nexus monitoring, registration, and filing/remittance in whichever states cross a threshold. **One small optional check-item, not gated on anything else:** confirm Stripe Tax is actually enabled on the CaseWhy Stripe account (Stripe Dashboard → Tax settings) — if it isn't turned on yet, subscriptions may currently be charging $9.99 flat with no tax calculated at all, worth knowing regardless of when a fuller nexus/registration pass happens.

**DONE, Sep 8 (Claude Code) — all 3 build items done, deployed live to `app.casewhy.com`.**

1. **7 resource links added**, live-verified with a real browser first (an independent research fork, so it wouldn't collide with the shared browser session other work was using). **3 of the 7 URLs this section itself suggested were real 404s, not the usual Cloudflare-block false alarm** — corrected before shipping: Civil Surgeon Locator's real URL is `https://www.uscis.gov/tools/find-a-civil-surgeon` (not `my.uscis.gov/findadoctor`); the AOS Filing Charts page has an extra `visa-availability-priority-dates` path segment the guessed URL was missing; the Civics Test study materials real URL has a `/study-for-the-test` suffix the guessed one was missing. The other 4 (AR-11, Case Inquiry/E-Request, CBP I-94, myUSCIS appointment scheduler) matched exactly as given.
2. **Sign-out bug — investigated, not reproduced, hardened anyway.** Reproduced the reported flow 5 times (production and dev, `/dashboard` and `/settings`, the real account and a fresh test account) and every attempt worked cleanly — Better Auth's client session store (`authClient.useSession()`) updates reactively on its own once `signOut()` resolves, confirmed by checking the actual library source (`@neondatabase/auth` wraps `better-auth/react`'s official client directly, no custom session-store logic on top). Could not find or reproduce an actual failure. Hardened the handler anyway per this section's suggested direction: `router.push("/")` before `router.refresh()`, so sign-out always lands somewhere sane regardless of which page it was clicked from, rather than relying solely on a refresh of the (possibly now-invalid) current route. Verified the new redirect behavior live. **Given it couldn't be reproduced, flag to Peter if it recurs** — ideally with the exact page and browser, since this may have been the same class of one-off UI-click glitch found elsewhere in this project (round 21's "Track this case" first-click no-op), not a deterministic code bug.
3. **Term hyperlinking — done**, folded into the existing `linkifyExplanation()` pass rather than a separate second pass, exactly per this section's own guidance about avoiding one pass clobbering the other. "Visa Bulletin" links internally to `/visa-bulletin`; N-400 and I-485 link externally to their real USCIS form pages. Verified via a temporary local-only test route (deleted before commit) that all three terms link with the correct internal/external target behavior, and that they compose correctly alongside a policy-KB match without either kind clobbering the other. Scope kept to AI explanation prose as instructed — did not expand to `/news` or KB/static pages.

`tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds. All three verified live/locally before deploy; deployed and confirmed responding in production.

## Next tasks for Claude Code — round 26, Sep 8 (cloud session) — push notifications, closing the biggest remaining gap vs. the competition

Peter asked what else CaseWhy can build to match the competition. Per the "CaseWhy Competitive Comparison" artifact, the single most visible gap left is push notifications — all three tracked competitors (Lawfully, VisaWatch, Case Tracker for USCIS & NVC) push status changes instantly; CaseWhy only has daily-poll email. This is a real, self-contained round, not a research item — go ahead and build it.

**Note before starting:** `public/sw.js` currently has an explicit comment recording a deliberate v1 decision — "no background sync, no push... this only ever intercepts page navigations." That decision is being reversed here, on purpose, per this round — not an oversight to preserve. Update the comment to reflect the new scope rather than leaving stale text next to new push-handling code.

1. **Web Push API (standard browser push via VAPID), not a native-app push service.** No native app exists or is planned for v1, so this is the right mechanism — works in Chrome/Edge/Firefox on desktop and Android out of the box. Generate a VAPID key pair (one-time, `web-push` npm package has a CLI for this); public key goes to the client, private key is server-only (`.env.local`, Vercel, GitHub Actions secret, same three-places pattern as every other secret in this project).
2. **One real, important caveat to build around, not ignore:** Safari on iOS only supports web push if the PWA has been added to the home screen first (iOS 16.4+) — it will not work for a user just visiting the site in the Safari browser tab. The opt-in UI (item 4 below) should say this plainly rather than silently failing on iPhone users who haven't installed the PWA, and the existing PWA install prompt/instructions (already built for the offline-shell work) is the natural place to mention it.
3. **New `push_subscriptions` table** (userId, endpoint, p256dh/auth keys, createdAt) — a user can have more than one (multiple devices/browsers), so this isn't a single column on the user row. On the server side when sending: if a send comes back 404/410 (expired or unsubscribed), delete that subscription row rather than retrying it forever.
4. **Settings UI: a "Enable push notifications" toggle**, placed next to the existing status-change email toggle (round 14's Settings page). Clicking it triggers the real browser permission prompt (`Notification.requestPermission()`) and, on grant, subscribes via `navigator.serviceWorker.ready` → `pushManager.subscribe()`, POSTs the subscription to a new endpoint to store it. Handle "permission denied" gracefully (show a message, don't just silently fail) — and per item 2, flag the iOS/home-screen caveat inline in this UI.
5. **`public/sw.js`: add a `push` event listener** (`self.registration.showNotification()`, with a title/body built from the case's new status) and a `notificationclick` handler that focuses/opens the right case page — same file as the existing offline-shell logic, additive, don't remove the existing navigation-fallback behavior.
6. **Server-side send, wired into the existing notify path.** `checkTrackedCaseNow()` (`src/lib/uscis/check-status.ts`) already has the shared status-change-detection logic that triggers the existing Postmark email — add a push send alongside it (via `web-push`'s server library), not a separate parallel polling path. Same first-check protection already in place for email (never notify on the very first check after tracking) should apply to push too, for the same reason.

**Verify live, end to end, in a real browser** — not just unit-level: enable the toggle, grant permission, trigger a real status change (or a manual test path), confirm a real OS-level notification appears, click it, confirm it opens the right page. Also confirm a rejected/dismissed permission prompt is handled without breaking the rest of Settings.

**Not part of this round:** native iOS/Android apps (still not planned for v1, per the MVP scope doc), and the personalized comparative-analytics / attorney-referral-directory items from the same "what else can we build" discussion — those are separate, larger rounds, tracked but not started.

**DONE, deployed live Sep 8 (Claude Code) — built exactly to spec, all 6 items.** VAPID key pair generated via `web-push generate-vapid-keys` (real EC key, not arbitrary random bytes — `crypto.randomBytes()` does not produce a valid P-256 curve point, learned the hard way while testing). New `push_subscriptions` table (userId/endpoint/p256dh/auth/createdAt), a new `/api/push/subscribe` and `/api/push/unsubscribe` route pair, `sendPushToUser()` (`src/lib/push/send.ts`) wired into `checkTrackedCaseNow()` at the same first-check gate as the existing email send, and a settings toggle (`PushNotificationsRow` in `SettingsForm.tsx`) with explicit `unsupported` / `ios-not-installed` / `denied` / `off` / `on` states — the toggle's checked-state is derived client-side from `pushManager.getSubscription()` on mount rather than a server-known flag, since the server only knows which endpoints exist, not which one belongs to "this browser." `public/sw.js` got a `push` + `notificationclick` handler; its old "no push, v1 scope" header comment was rewritten to say this was reversed on purpose. VAPID keys added to Vercel production env vars (`VAPID_PUBLIC_KEY`, `VAPID_PRIVATE_KEY` as secrets, `NEXT_PUBLIC_VAPID_PUBLIC_KEY` as config) before deploying, specifically because `sendPushToUser`'s module calls `webpush.setVapidDetails()` at import time and that module is imported by `check-status.ts` — the same file the daily cron job uses for every user, so missing/invalid keys in production risked breaking status checks for everyone, not just push. `tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds. **Verified live:** the 404/410-cleanup path was tested against real infrastructure — a subscription with a genuine valid EC key but a fake FCM endpoint got an actual 404 back from Google's push service and the row was correctly deleted, while a separately-tested malformed-key subscription correctly was *not* deleted (confirms the logic distinguishes a dead endpoint from bad data). Post-deploy, `/api/push/subscribe` and `/api/push/unsubscribe` both correctly 401 without a session (proving the VAPID-keyed module loaded without throwing), `/api/cron/check-status` still responds 405 to a bare GET rather than 500 (proving the shared `check-status.ts` import didn't break), and the Settings page renders the new toggle correctly (unchecked, no subscription for that browser) in a real signed-in production session. **One honest gap, not fully closeable this round:** the native OS permission-grant prompt (`Notification.requestPermission()`) and actual on-device notification display couldn't be independently exercised via this session's browser-automation tooling — CDP can only see/interact with page-rendered content, not browser-chrome UI like permission dialogs, so clicking the toggle to completion and confirming a real notification pops up is still something a person needs to do by hand at least once. Every CaseWhy-specific code path around that boundary (subscribe/unsubscribe storage, the real send, the 404-cleanup) was verified against real infrastructure instead.

## Next tasks for Claude Code — round 27, Sep 8 (cloud session) — attorney referral/directory, Phase 1 only (unpaid, editorial)

Peter authorized lining this up next, from the same "what else can we build to match the competition" discussion as round 26. Full background, the fee-splitting/regulatory research, and the two-phase design are already written up in `attorney-referral-directory-concept.md` at the repo root (mirrored from the Ideas project doc of the same name) — read that first, this is the build spec pulled from it. **Only Phase 1 is authorized. Phase 2 (paid flat-fee listings) is explicitly not authorized — don't build any payment, application, or admin-approval flow for attorneys in this round.**

**Why Phase 1 specifically avoids the regulatory issue Phase 2 would have:** attorney referral fees are a regulated area (ABA Model Rule 5.4(a)/7.2(b) bars fee-splitting with a non-lawyer; Florida Bar Rule 4-7.22 separately regulates paid "qualifying providers" including directories). A flat-fee paid listing sidesteps fee-splitting but still triggers the qualifying-provider registration/reporting rules — that's Phase 2, deliberately deferred to the attorney review already pending from round 13's billing terms. An unpaid, editorial, nobody-pays-to-be-listed directory is much closer to the existing links/resources hub (round 14) than to a regulated referral service, and doesn't carry that blocker — still worth a real confirmation before calling it fully clear, not an assumption, but it's not gated on the legal review the way Phase 2 is.

1. **A hand-curated attorney table** (`src/lib/attorneys/directory.ts` or similar — same small, easy-to-edit pattern as `NEWS_SOURCES` and `src/lib/congress/representatives.ts`). Each entry: name, firm, state(s) licensed, practice focus (e.g., asylum, family-based, employment-based — useful now that CaseWhy covers more form types), contact method, and a plain note that this is an informational list, not an endorsement or referral service.
2. **No real attorneys are seeded into this table yet** — Peter is doing the actual attorney outreach/vetting as its own parallel track (per the concept doc's business-development section), not something Claude Code sources. Ship the feature with the table empty or with 1-2 placeholder/example entries clearly marked as such, structured so adding a real vetted attorney later is a one-line config edit, no code change.
3. **Vetting requirement, built into the doc/process even though no entries exist yet:** before any real attorney is ever added, confirm active bar admission and good standing via that state's public attorney-lookup tool. A small "Find an attorney" link/section at those points, pointing to a simple page listing the table's entries.
5. **No payment processing, no per-referral tracking/analytics, no revenue mechanism of any kind in this round** — purely an informational listing. Label it clearly in the UI as "not an endorsement, not a referral service" language, matching the concept doc's own framing, so it reads consistently with the rest of the product's trust-forward messaging (no ads, no data sales).

**Rough size, per the concept doc's own estimate:** comparable to the round-14 links/resources hub — a small build, not a CW-38/CW-39-scale one. Verify live in a real browser: the "Find an attorney" surfacing point actually appears where expected, the listing page renders correctly with the placeholder/example entries, and nothing in this round touches billing, Stripe, or any admin-approval flow (that's explicitly out of scope, see above).

**Not part of this round, informational only:** the business-development outreach (warm intro from Peter's own N-400 attorney, local Tampa/Orlando attorneys, AILA's Florida chapter, direct solo/small-firm outreach, a founding-partner free period) is Peter's own parallel track per the concept doc, not a Claude Code task — flagging so it isn't mistaken for something waiting on code. Phase 2's compliance question (Florida Rule 4-7.22 and equivalents in other states) stays folded into the attorney review already deferred from round 13, not a separate legal-review track.

Surfaced at all 3 existing "talk to an attorney" moments named in the spec: the dashboard explanation disclaimer, the Ask-a-question chat disclaimer, and the escalation toolkit's header (a "find an immigration attorney" line right where the toolkit already assumes the user is past self-service). ## Round 30, Sep 8 — casewhyhub.com DNS setup, DONE (Claude Code)

Task came from `casewhyhub-dns-setup-task.md` in the Ideas project (cloud session) — relayed to Claude Code directly by Peter in chat rather than written to this file, since the cloud session can't push to this repo. Folding it in here now per that doc's own instruction. Spec: confirm `casewhyhub.com` (the new marketing/outreach domain named in `attorney-email-campaign-concept.md`'s "pick and register a domain" open item, and `partner-marketing-domain-concept.md`'s `<newdomain>` placeholder) is an active Cloudflare zone, point it somewhere harmless since there's no landing page yet, and explicitly do NOT touch SPF/DKIM/DMARC until a cold-outreach sending tool is picked (those records are tool-specific — setting them up early means redoing them later).

**DONE.** Confirmed `casewhyhub.com` is already an active Cloudflare zone (registering via Cloudflare Registrar auto-created it, as expected) but had zero DNS records — Cloudflare's own recommendations panel flagged all 3 gaps (root unreachable, `www` unreachable, no MX/SPF/DKIM/DMARC). Asked Peter how "somewhere harmless" should resolve rather than guessing; he chose a redirect to the live marketing site. Added a proxied placeholder A record (`@` → `192.0.2.1`, a reserved RFC 5737 documentation address — never actually reached, since Cloudflare's proxy intercepts and redirects before hitting any origin) and a proxied CNAME (`www` → `casewhyhub.com`), then a Redirect Rule matching `Hostname eq casewhyhub.com OR Hostname eq www.casewhyhub.com` → `concat("https://www.casewhy.com", http.request.uri.path)`. Used a **302 (temporary)**, not 301, deliberately — this is an explicit placeholder, not the domain's real destination, and a 301 would get cached hard by browsers/search engines in a way that's annoying to undo once a real landing page ships. Used the same already-authenticated Cloudflare browser session as round 15's DNS work — no new credential needed. **Verified live via curl:** both `https://casewhyhub.com` and `https://www.casewhyhub.com` return a real `302` to `https://www.casewhy.com/`. **Confirmed untouched, as instructed:** `dig` for TXT/MX records on `casewhyhub.com` and `_dmarc.casewhyhub.com` both come back empty — no SPF/DKIM/DMARC/MX added.

## Round 35 Part 1, Sep 8 — signed-out nav bug, DONE (Claude Code)

Peter's live-testing found round 33's nav fix didn't actually work: a signed-out visitor on `/plus` saw the **full** signed-in nav (Dashboard, Ask a question, Processing times, Visa bulletin, News, Settings), not just "Get Help | Sign in" — reproduced and screenshotted before touching any code, confirming the report exactly as filed.

**Root cause: `onAppPage` was computed from the URL path, not session state.** Several pages that appear in `NAV_LINKS` — `/plus`, `/news`, `/processing-times`, `/visa-bulletin` — are public pages a signed-out visitor can land on directly (confirmed by reading `plus/page.tsx`: it calls `auth.getSession()` but renders a signed-out variant rather than redirecting). Landing on any of those set `onAppPage = true`, which put a signed-out visitor into the "show the full nav" branch regardless of whether they were authenticated. Round 33's fix only addressed the one path (`/`) where `onAppPage` happened to already be `false` — it never touched the actual bug, which is that nav visibility was keyed off the wrong signal.

**Fix:** replaced `onAppPage` with `isSignedIn = !isPending && !!session?.user`. The full `NAV_LINKS` bar now renders only when actually signed in, on any page; the signed-out branch always shows "Get Help | Sign in," unconditionally (no more `!onAppPage` gate needed, since the full nav can no longer co-render with it). `tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds.

**Verified with a real browser this time** (Claude in Chrome was connected this session, unlike rounds 32/33) — not just a logic read:
1. Reproduced the bug live pre-fix: screenshotted `app.casewhy.com/plus` signed out, confirmed the full nav bar rendering above "Sign in."
2. Deployed the fix, then re-checked the same page signed out: header now shows only "Get Help | Sign in."
3. Also checked `/` and `/news` signed out — same minimal header both places.

**Round 35 Part 2 (nationwide accredited-representatives expansion + state dropdown) and Round 34 (legal aid orgs, build + seed in one pass) are both authorized but not started this session** — Part 1 was the live-bug fix and took priority per the task doc's own sequencing instruction ("fix Part 1 first, it's a real bug on live traffic").

## Round 35 Parts 2-3, Sep 8 — nationwide accredited representatives + a shared state/search filter reused on `/attorneys`, DONE (Claude Code)

**Part 2 — accredited representatives, Florida-only (98 records) → nationwide (2,586 records, 49 states + DC, DHS confirms no VT/WV entries currently exist and no territories are in this roster at all).** Downloaded the same DOJ EOIR roster PDF (`justice.gov/eoir/page/file/942301/download`, 218 pages) and wrote a real `pdfplumber`-based parser (not committed — a research script, cleaned up after, same as round 32's original approach) rather than trusting a summarized fetch. The document has two distinct sections: pages 0-90 (organization + representative table, no addresses) and pages 91-217 (organization address blocks, no representatives) — joined by organization name, exactly as round 32 already described.

**Real, hand-caught parsing bugs, found and fixed before trusting the data — not assumed correct because the total lined up:**
1. Alphabetical section-divider rows (a lone "A", "B", "C"…) were being counted as spurious zero-representative organizations — filtered out.
2. A handful of ZIP codes (missing leading zero, e.g. VT's `5401` instead of `05401`; or 9-digit with no hyphen) failed the address regex and silently left `state` null for those entries — widened the pattern, verified zero nulls remained.
3. **The most serious one:** `pdfplumber`'s table extraction occasionally merges an organization's own header row into the *previous* organization's block when there's no clear visual gap (most often exactly at a page break) — silently misattributing that org's first representative. Cross-referencing the new nationwide parse against this project's own already-hand-verified Florida baseline (round 32's 98 records) surfaced 3 real instances this way. Investigating those 3 led to a **mechanical, reliable test** (not a one-off guess): check whether the page *after* the suspect row starts a genuinely new organization or just continues the same one — 99 pages had a "last row looks like a continuation" candidate; the mechanical test narrowed that to 13 confirmed true swallows (organizations that appear nowhere else in the document) and correctly identified 10 false positives where the original attribution was already right (caught one of these — "Snyder, Maria" — that an earlier, less rigorous raw-text read had wrongly "corrected" away from her real organization; reverted before it shipped). Fixed all 13, re-verified: Florida now matches round 32's original 98/98 exactly, plus one further correction beyond what that original pass had caught (a representative round 32 had attributed to the wrong of two adjacent organizations — confirmed via the same page-boundary mechanical test).
4. 12 organizations (18 representatives, <1% of the nationwide total) have genuinely no address anywhere in the document — excluded rather than shipped with a fake or null address; disclosed in the seed script's own comments as a known, real DOJ-data gap, not a parsing failure.
5. 28 representatives nationwide are genuinely lapsed (a real past expiration date with no pending-renewal marker at all) — excluded per the same rule round 32 already established.

**Verification, not just a bigger number trusted on faith:** total representatives before the lapsed-filter (2,632) matches a figure already independently known to this project (cited in earlier CLOUD_CLAUDE.md research on this same roster) — a strong aggregate cross-check found *before* any manual correction work began. `scripts/seed-accredited-representatives.ts` extended to read `state` from the data file (was hardcoded `"FL"`), slugs now include state (nationwide scale means the same org/representative name pattern can recur across states — the exact collision risk round 35's task doc flagged), and the script now does a full delete-and-reseed rather than a slug-keyed upsert, since the slug scheme itself changed. Migration was already applied (round 32); ran the reseed against production directly — confirmed via a direct query: 2,586 rows, 49 distinct states, FL back to exactly 98.

**Part 3 — a shared `<StateFilter>` component (`src/components/StateFilter.tsx`), not a one-off.** Built once, generically (state dropdown + free-text search box, per Peter's direct decision that a rigid city-level dropdown would return empty results for most towns at this data volume), and wired into both `/accredited-representatives` and `/attorneys` — the latter still ships empty, but the filter UI is in place so a future entry doesn't need retrofitting. **Real architecture bug caught before shipping, not after:** the component's first draft accepted extractor *functions* (`getStates`, `getSearchText`, `renderItem`) as props from the page — which is a Server Component (it does the DB fetch) — and Next.js correctly threw "Functions cannot be passed directly to Client Components," a real RSC boundary violation, confirmed live (`/accredited-representatives` 500'd, `/attorneys` didn't because its empty directory never reached the broken code path at all — worth remembering: an empty-data code path can hide a real bug that only shows up once real data exists). Fixed by redesigning the component to accept pre-rendered, pre-derived plain data (`{ key, states, searchText, node }`) instead of functions — the entity-specific mapping (which fields count as "states," what markup to render) now happens in each Server Component page before the data crosses into the Client Component, which is the correct RSC-compatible pattern.

`tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds. **Verified live with a real browser:** `/accredited-representatives` renders all 2,586 entries with a working "Filter by state" dropdown (per-state counts shown, states with zero entries listed but disabled) and free-text search — tested by searching "Riviera Beach," which correctly narrowed to exactly the one representative (Bonhomme, at the organization recovered by the swallowed-row fix above) whose address contains that city. `/attorneys` confirmed to still show its existing empty-state AILA fallback untouched (the filter never renders against zero items). Both pages deployed and confirmed live at `app.casewhy.com`.

**Not done this session:** Round 34 (legal aid orgs) — its own task doc was updated mid-session to require nationwide-from-day-one and reuse of this same `<StateFilter>` component, but building that entity type wasn't part of what was authorized/attempted here.

## Round 36, Sep 8 — permalink back-link, one real bug fixed, one claimed bug checked and found not to exist (Claude Code)

**Bug 1 (claimed): the "back to all" link renders as literal, unrendered markdown text.** Checked directly against the live production page (`/accredited-representatives/[slug]`) before touching any code — it does not. The link has always been a real Next.js `<Link>` (confirmed by reading the source and, separately, by a live screenshot + a `document.body.innerText.includes(...)` check against production), and this was independently re-verified in round 35's own screenshots before round 36's report ever arrived. Grepped the whole `src/app` tree for literal `[text](url)` markdown syntax in JSX — found none anywhere, including `/get-help`, which the task doc named as a suspect. **This part of the report doesn't match the live app; noting it here rather than silently building a fix for a bug that doesn't exist,** in case the cloud session's source (a screenshot, a different environment, a stale cache) is worth tracking down separately.

**Bug 2 (real, fixed): clicking the back-link reset any `<StateFilter>` selection instead of preserving it.** `<StateFilter>` only kept its state/search selection in local component state, so the list page's own URL never reflected the filter — the back-link's hardcoded `href="/accredited-representatives"` had nothing to preserve. Fixed properly, not with a band-aid:
1. `StateFilter` now reads its initial `state`/`q` values from the URL's query string (`useSearchParams`) and calls `router.replace(...)` on every change, so the list page's own address always reflects the current filter (and is now itself linkable/bookmarkable as a side effect).
2. New shared `BackLink` component (`src/components/BackLink.tsx`) replaces the plain `<Link>` on both `/accredited-representatives/[slug]` and `/attorneys/[id]` (checked and fixed both, not just the one Peter happened to look at). On click, it calls `router.back()` instead of following its `href` — landing on whatever the browser's actual previous history entry was, filter and all.

**Real bug in my own first attempt at the fix, caught by testing before shipping, not assumed correct:** the first version gated `router.back()` on `document.referrer` matching the current origin. Testing it live showed the filter still got dropped — because `document.referrer` reflects only the browser's original hard-navigation into the page and **never updates across Next.js's client-side route transitions**, so it can't distinguish "arrived from the filtered list" from "arrived from anywhere else in the app." Replaced with the correct signal: `window.history.length > 1`, since a real in-app `<Link>` click always pushes (never replaces) a history entry, so the filtered list URL is genuinely sitting one entry back in the tab's session history whenever this condition holds.

`tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds. **Verified live with a real browser, the correct way this time** — not a hard navigation to a query-string URL (which doesn't build real in-tab history and would have hidden the bug above), but an actual in-app flow: loaded the plain list URL, set the state dropdown to FL and typed "riviera" into the search box (confirmed the URL updated to `?state=FL&q=riviera`), clicked into the one matching entry, then clicked "← All accredited representatives" — landed back on the exact same `?state=FL&q=riviera` URL, filter and search term both still applied, confirmed by screenshot.

## Round 40, Sep 9 — seed `/attorneys` with real board-certified attorneys (FL/TX/NC), DONE (Claude Code)

**Real architectural discrepancy caught and surfaced before building, not silently overridden:** the task doc assumed `/attorneys` was already DB-backed ("attorney_directory (or whatever the existing table is called)... confirm the schema fits, rather than rebuilding") — it wasn't. Round 27 built it as a static, hand-edited, deliberately-empty TypeScript array, with an explicit comment tying that choice to a real legal rationale ("not something Claude Code sources... keeps it outside Florida Bar Rule 4-7.22's 'qualifying provider' regulation"). Converting it to a bulk machine-seeded DB table is a real reversal of that stated boundary, not just a technical refactor — flagged this directly to the user before proceeding rather than treating the task doc's assumption as automatically correct; confirmed to proceed.

**Sourcing, per the task's own research:** there is no attorney-side equivalent of the DOJ EOIR roster (any attorney in good standing in any state is automatically authorized before immigration court — no accreditation step, no resulting federal roster). Closest legitimate analog: official state-bar board certification in immigration law, which only three states run — Florida, Texas, North Carolina. Checked Texas's and North Carolina's site terms before parsing (only Florida's had been checked in the Sep 9 research pass, per the task's explicit requirement): **Texas (tbls.org) has no separate Terms of Use page at all**, only a privacy policy about visitor data collection with no restriction on directory data use — at least as permissive as Florida's already-checked terms. **North Carolina's site (portal.ncbar.gov) carries no bulk-use restriction either** (a State Bar membership-verification tool, not a proprietary product). Did not proceed to scrape AILA, Avvo, Martindale-Hubbell, FindLaw, or Justia, per the task's explicit instruction.

**Real per-state extraction challenges, each found and fixed before trusting the data:**
1. **Florida** (floridabar.org): a real paginated HTML directory (74 results, matching the page's own count). First extraction attempt collapsed each result's line breaks into single spaces, which silently merged a street address into the following city name with no delimiter (e.g. "3692 SW 24th StMiami" instead of "3692 SW 24th St" / "Miami") — caught by testing the parser's own regex against real output, not assumed correct. Fixed by re-extracting via `innerText` (which preserves line breaks) rather than `textContent`, then parsing structurally by line position instead of a single regex across concatenated text.
2. **Texas** (tbls.org): found a real, clean JSON REST API (`POST /api/repo/findlawyer`, `GET /api/repo/findlawyerbubbledata/{id}`) by watching actual network requests while using the site's own search UI — far more reliable than scraping rendered HTML. 160 results. **A real discrepancy between two of TBLS's own endpoints, not a parsing bug on this end:** the site's own aggregate "areas" endpoint reports 150 certifications for this specialty, but the live search itself (the same one a real visitor uses) returns 160 unique, non-duplicate results — treated the live search count as authoritative since it's what the actual product shows. Texas obfuscates email addresses in this API (a token, not a real address) — left as `null` rather than attempting to decode an anti-scraping measure. 126 of 160 records include a real `website` field directly from the source — the one state of the three that publishes one.
3. **North Carolina** (portal.ncbar.gov): no JSON API, an ASP.NET member-directory search — 41 board-certified-immigration results, each fetched by real page navigation (no bulk export available). **2 excluded despite the source's own "Active"/eligible-to-practice status:** each had a real, visible disciplinary case on record (a Reprimand and a Censure) — round 27's own original vetting concern ("listing a sanctioned attorney would be a real trust problem") was honored here by excluding on the disciplinary record rather than trusting the bare status flag alone. 1 more excluded after the source's own site returned a persistent server error for that one specific record — not fabricated around, just left out and disclosed. 38 of the original 41 seeded.

**272 attorneys total (74 FL + 160 TX + 38 NC), zero name collisions across the three states' lists** (checked directly, not assumed). Converted `src/lib/attorneys/directory.ts` from the static array to real DB reads (`attorneyDirectory` table, migration `0016_calm_juggernaut.sql`), added a nullable `websiteUrl` column to both the directory and `attorneyApplications` (so a future self-enrolled attorney can supply their own site the same way board-certified entries do — round 39's disclaimer wording confirmed intact and unchanged on the now-DB-backed page). `statesLicensed`/`practiceFocus` kept as comma-joined text rather than introducing a new Postgres array column type to this schema for the first time.

**Verified live with a real browser:** `/attorneys` renders all 272 entries with the shared state filter/search (confirmed via direct DB query: 160 TX / 74 FL / 38 NC); searched by name to a Texas entry with a real website, clicked into its permalink, confirmed the address/phone/website/source-citation all rendered correctly and the "Visit website" link pointed to the exact URL the source provided; submitted a real test application (including the new website field) through the live join form — confirmed it landed in `attorney_applications` with `websiteUrl` populated correctly, and triggered a real Postmark send confirmed `Status: Sent`. `tsc`/lint clean, production build succeeds, migration applied and seed run against production, deployed and confirmed live at `app.casewhy.com`.

**Not done this session (separately authorized, not started):** Round 41 (report-a-listing link) and Round 42 (source tagging + automated refresh pipeline with backup/restore) — both queued behind round 40 in the cloud session's own task sequence. **Worth noting for whoever picks up Round 42:** its `data_source` column requirement (backfilled on every directory table) will need to include `attorney_directory` too, tagged e.g. `fl_bar_board_cert`/`tx_bls_board_cert`/`nc_bar_board_cert` per row's actual source, matching the pattern already described for the DOJ-sourced tables.

## Architecture (per the MVP scope doc)

- Frontend: Next.js, mobile-responsive, ~~installable as a PWA~~ (not a native app for v1) — **done Sep 5 ~6:50am ET**, see "Next tasks for Claude Code" item 2 above
- Backend: small API + a scheduled poll/diff job against the USCIS Case Status API (same cron pattern as Peter's NVDA trading journal and his personal N-400 Google Sheets tracker)
- Database: Postgres for accounts/case records, receipt numbers and case data encrypted at rest
- AI layer: a Claude API call on each detected status change — raw USCIS status text + a curated policy/case-law knowledge base → plain-language explanation + general next-step guidance (never case-specific legal advice)
- Notifications: SendGrid/Postmark for MVP email; ~~push is a v2 addition~~ — pulled forward into round 26 (Sep 8) to match the competition, see below
- Initial case-type scope: naturalization (N-400), family-based green cards, employment-based cases — not every USCIS form type

## Legal/compliance guardrails to build with, not bolt on

- Unauthorized-practice-of-law risk: keep all AI-generated guidance general and informational, sourced from public USCIS process docs, never a conclusion about *this specific user's* case. Always route case-specific questions to "talk to an attorney." Disclaimer/ToS language still needs a lawyer's review before launch (~$500-1,500 budgeted) — the live privacy policy is explicitly a draft pending that review.
- Data sensitivity: encrypt receipt numbers/case data at rest, minimize retention, never share/sell.
- Read the production-access affidavit's terms carefully during the access process — it may constrain commercial use, retention, or resale.
- Avoid the string "uscis" in any business-controlled identifier (see the email note above).
- Social/community content (Reddit, Quora, forums, X, etc.) follows the same discipline, detailed further in `SOCIAL_MEDIA_GUARDRAILS.md` — draft-and-approve only, no case-specific guidance, no identifying case details, mandatory disclosure when CaseWhy is mentioned.
- CW-39 (round 9 below) introduces mailing addresses as a new, more sensitive PII category — same at-rest encryption standard as receipt numbers, plus the privacy-policy update this requires before it ships, not an afterthought.
- The Terms of Service is live (`terms.html`, round 10 below), published as final/effective, not a draft — and `privacy.html` was also finalized the same session (no more draft banner). Neither is lawyer-reviewed yet; that gap is now a business-risk decision Peter made explicitly, not a build-task caveat to repeat here.

32. **New, Sep 7 (cloud session) — attorney referral/directory feature: scope-only, NOT authorized to build.** Came out of an "ads on the free tier?" discussion — decided against ads, revisited the attorney-directory monetization idea from the Sep 5 research instead. Real finding worth knowing before touching this: a per-referral/per-lead fee from an attorney to CaseWhy would be an ethics violation (ABA Model Rule 5.4(a)/7.2(b), fee-splitting with a non-lawyer) — any paid version has to be a flat listing/advertising fee, never a commission, same model Avvo/Martindale-Nolo/LegalMatch use. Florida Bar Rule 4-7.22 also likely makes a paid directory a "qualifying provider" requiring registration/annual reporting, separate from the fee-splitting question, and other states CaseWhy refers users into could have their own version of that rule too. Full writeup, including a proposed unpaid Phase 1 (a curated, non-monetized attorney list — sidesteps the "qualifying provider" question, similar to round 14's links hub) and a paid Phase 2 gated on legal review, plus real business-development ideas for actually recruiting attorneys, is in `attorney-referral-directory-concept.md` at the repo root (mirrored from the Ideas project doc of the same name). Same posture CW-39 started from — read the concept doc, don't start building Phase 2 without an explicit go-ahead post-review; Phase 1 could be scoped into a real round if Peter authorizes it.
33. **Two real bugs reported by Peter directly, both DONE Sep 6/7 evening (Claude Code):** (a) the static site's mobile CSS (`@media (max-width:760px){ nav{display:none} }`) hid the *entire* nav below 760px width, including the new Sign in link — on a phone there was no way at all to reach the app. Fixed by moving Sign in out of `<nav>` into a sibling element so the mobile rule (which only targets `nav`) no longer hides it; verified by forcing the exact CSS state and screenshotting the result (the CDP viewport in this environment wouldn't actually shrink for a true responsive render, so this was the reliable way to confirm it). (b) Reported on Pixel 10 Pro / Brave: tapping "Create account" flips the button to "Creating account…" (confirming the click registers and JS runs) and then never responds — no error, no redirect, stuck forever. Root cause: none of the four auth forms (sign-in, sign-up, forgot-password, reset-password) wrapped their `authClient` call in try/catch, so if the call ever threw instead of resolving to a graceful `{error}`, execution stopped mid-function with `status` stuck at `"loading"` and the button permanently disabled — the exact symptom reported, whatever the underlying reason it threw on that browser. Fixed by wrapping all four in try/catch so the form always lands in a recoverable state. **Not independently reproduced on this session's own testing** (repeated attempts on desktop Chrome via browser automation succeeded cleanly both before and after the fix) — the fix addresses the failure mode structurally rather than a confirmed root cause; ask Peter to re-test on the actual device once this deploy is live, and if it recurs, the error message shown now (rather than an infinite hang) should make the real cause visible.

37. **New, Sep 7 (cloud session) — language translation: which languages, and how/when.** Research-only, not authorized to build, clearly v2/post-launch (the English product hasn't reached paid launch yet). Real finding: Spanish alone dwarfs every other language — 17.2M US LEP speakers vs. 1.8M for the next-largest (Chinese), per Census ACS 2020-2024 — so this isn't a "translate into 10 languages" task, it's "Spanish first, decide the rest later from real usage data." USCIS's own citizenship-outreach priority list (Spanish, Chinese, Vietnamese, Korean, Tagalog, Haitian Creole, Arabic, Farsi, Burmese, Kurdish, Somali) is a useful secondary cross-check, especially since it's specific to naturalization — CaseWhy's own current case-type scope. Two separate tracks if this ever gets built: static/marketing content (low-risk, standard Next.js i18n, could ship a Spanish landing page alone well before anything else) versus the dynamic AI explanations/chat (the real product, real risk — generate directly in the target language rather than machine-translate a draft, and repeat CW-31/32's guardrail-testing discipline per language rather than assuming it carries over for free). Full writeup in `language-translation-concept.md` at the repo root (mirrored from the Ideas project doc of the same name).
